## 1. Administrative/regulatory information and recommendations on the procedure

## 1.1. Submission of the dossier

On 02 May 2025, Biocryst Ireland Limited submitted a group of variations consisting of an extension of the marketing authorisation and the following variations:

| Variation(s) requested   | Variation(s) requested                                                                                                                                                                                    | Type   | Annexes affected   |
|--------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------|--------------------|
| C.I.4                    | C.I HUMAN AND VETERINARY MEDICINAL PRODUCTS - C.I.4 Change(s) in the Summary of Product Characteristics, Labelling or Package Leaflet due to new quality, preclinical, clinical or pharmacovigilance data | II     | I, IIIA, IIIB, A   |

Extension application to introduce a new pharmaceutical form associated with new strengths (78 mg, 96 mg, 108 mg and 132 mg film - coated granules). The new presentations are indicated to include treatment for paediatric patients aged 2 to less than 12 years. The extension application is grouped with a type II clinical variation (C.I.4). As a consequence, sections 4.1, 4.2, 4.4, 4.8, 5.1 and 5.2 of the SmPC are updated. The Package Leaflet and Labelling are updated in accordance. Version 2.1 of the RMP has also been submitted.

## 1.2. Legal basis and dossier content

## The legal basis for this application refers to:

Article 7.2 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 1234/2008 - Group of variations

## 1.3. Scientific advice and protocol assistance

Not applicable

## 1.4. Information on paediatrics

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 1901/2006, the application included an EMA Decision EMA/PE/0000266800 on the agreement of a paediatric investigation plan (PIP).

At the time of submission of the application, the PIP P/0151/2024 was not yet completed as some measures were deferred.  In addition, the PIP EMA/PE/0000266800 was completed.

The PDCO issued an opinion on compliance for the PIP EMA/PE/0000266800.

The  Paediatric  Committee  (PDCO)  issued  on  25  July  2025  a  positive  opinion  on  compliance  for  PIP EMA/PE/0000266800.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

All studies/measures contained in the PIP were checked for compliance:

| Agreed studies/measures (study identifier)   | Description                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
|----------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Study 1 (FORM-01)                            | Development of a multi-particulate dosage form coated to mask the flavour of the drug product for the paediatric population from 2 to less than 12 years of age                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Study 2 (BCX7353-302, APeX-2)                | Randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel group, 3-parts study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of two dose levels of berotralstat as an oral treatment for the prevention of attacks in paediatric patients from 12 to less than 18 years of age (and adults) with hereditary angioedema (HAE)                                                              |
| Study 3 (HAE) (BCX7353-204, APeX-S).         | Open-label study to evaluate the long-term safety of daily oral berotralstat in subjects with Type I and II hereditary angioedema in paediatric patients from 12 to less than 18 years of age (and adults) with hereditary angioedema                                                                                                                                        |
| Study 4 (BCX7353-304)                        | Open label study to evaluate the safety, pharmacokinetics, and effectiveness of berotralstat in paediatric patients from 2 to less than 12 years of age with hereditary angioedema for the prevention of attacks                                                                                                                                                             |
| Study 5 (BCX7353-PPK1)                       | Population PK modelling and simulation study to support extrapolation and evaluate the PK of berotralstat in the prevention of attacks in paediatric subjects with HAE from 2 to less than 12 years of age, to investigate the performance of the intended PK/PD study designs, and to provide dosing regimens based on adult and adolescent PK and efficacy via simulations |

## 1.5. Information on orphan market exclusivity

Not applicable

## 1.5.1. Similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products

Pursuant to Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No. 141/2000 and Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 847/2000, the MAH did submit a critical report addressing the possible similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products.

## 1.7. CHMP outcome

## 1.7.1. Considerations related to paediatrics

The requirements for the submitted dossier in relation to paediatrics are described in section 1.3.  of this report.

The CHMP reviewed the available paediatric data of studies subject to the agreed Paediatric Investigation Plan and the results of these studies are reflected in the Summary of Product Characteristics (SmPC) and, as appropriate, the Package Leaflet.

## 1.7.2. Considerations related to orphan market exclusivity

The requirements of the submitted dossier in relation to orphan market exclusivity are described in section 1.5. of this report.

## Similarity with authorised orphan medicinal products

The CHMP by consensus is of the opinion that Orladeyo is not similar to Ekterly and Takhzyro within the meaning of Article 3 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 847/2000. See the appendix on similarity.

## 1.7.3. Opinion

Based on the CHMP review of data on quality, safety and efficacy, the CHMP considers by consensus that the benefit-risk balance of Orladeyo 72 mg, 96 mg,108 mg and 132 mg, film-coated granules is favourable in the following indication:

Orladeyo is indicated for routine prevention of recurrent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in:

- children aged 2 to less than 12 years weighing at least 15 kg
- adolescents aged 12 and older weighing less than 40 kg

The CHMP therefore recommends the extension(s) of the marketing authorisation for Orladeyo, subject to the conditions described in the following sections.

In addition, the CHMP does recommend the variation to the terms of the marketing authorisation concerning the following changes:

| Variation(s) adopted   | Variation(s) adopted                                                                                                                                                                                      | Type              | Annexes affected    |
|------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------|---------------------|
| C.I.4                  | C.I HUMAN AND VETERINARY MEDICINAL PRODUCTS - C.I.4 Change(s) in the Summary of Product Characteristics, Labelling or Package Leaflet due to new quality, preclinical, clinical or pharmacovigilance data | Variation type II | I, IIIA, IIIB and A |

Extension application to introduce a new pharmaceutical form associated with new strengths (72 mg, 96 mg,108 and 132 film - coated granules). The new presentations are indicated to include treatment for paediatric patients aged 2 to less than 12 years weighing at least 15 kg and patients aged 12 years and older weighing less than 40 kg. The extension application is grouped with a type II clinical variation (C.I.4). In addition, the indication for the already authorised 150 mg capsules is modified to reflect that the capsules formulation is indicated in adult and adolescent patients aged 12 years and older weighing at least 40 kg. As a consequence, sections 4.1, 4.2, 4.4, 4.8, 5.1, 5.2 and 5.3 of the SmPC are updated. The Package Leaflet and Labelling are updated in accordance. Version 4.0 of the RMP has also been adopted.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 1.7.4. Other conditions and requirements of the marketing authorisation

## 1.7.4.1. Periodic safety update reports

The requirements for submission of periodic safety update reports for this medicinal product are set out in the list of Union reference dates (EURD list) provided for under Article 107c(7) of Directive 2001/83/EC and any subsequent updates published on the European medicines web-portal.

## 1.7.5. Conditions or restrictions with regard to the safe and effective use of the medicinal product

## 2. Introduction

## Therapeutic Context

Hereditary angioedema (HAE) is a genetically determined disease of dysregulation of the contact activation pathway. HAE is characterized clinically by recurrent episodes of angioedema of the skin, pharynx, larynx, gastrointestinal tract, genitals, and extremities.

## Aetiology

The great majority of HAE cases are caused by mutations in SERPING1, the gene for the major regulator of the contact activation pathway, complement 1 esterase inhibitor (C1-INH), leading to dysregulation of bradykinin (BK). Insufficiency of C1-INH results in abnormally upregulated cleavage of high-molecular-weight kininogen (HK) by plasma kallikrein, releasing BK, which mediates angioedema. HAE due to C1-INH deficiency (C1-INH-HAE) is a rare autosomal dominant condition, with approximately ¾ of cases familial and ¼ arising from new mutations in SERPING1.

HAE types I and II, are characterized by impaired C1 inhibitor (C1-INH) activity (HAE-C1-INH) due to protein deficiency (type I) or dysfunction (type II).

In patients with HAE, mutations in the regulatory or coding regions of the C1-INH gene results in either a failure to produce C1-INH or in the production of nonfunctional C1-INH. As a result, kallikrein production and activity is induced by even asymptomatic levels of vascular endothelial contact activation in patents with HAE. Plasma HK levels may be lower than normal and BK is detectable in asymptomatic patients with HAE. Symptomatic attacks, which may be triggered by trauma, infection, changes in hormone levels (e.g., with oral contraceptive use or with the menstrual changes), or emotional stress, are characterized by uncontrolled contact activation, with increased kallikrein production, HK cleavage, and dramatic increases in circulating BK.

More recently, hereditary mutations of other proteins involved in the contact activation pathway have been identified in individuals with similar clinical phenotypes but normal C1-INH levels. Causal mutations have been identified in Factor XII, plasminogen, angiopoietin-1, kininogen, myoferlin, and HS3ST6, collectively termed HAE with normal C1-INH. To date, these conditions have been reported in only very limited numbers. In Japan, &lt; 2% of HAE cases appear to be in the category of HAE with normal C1-INH. Several features suggest that HAE-nl-C1 INH, like C1 INH-HAE, may be BK mediated, including the lack of response to antihistamines, corticosteroids, and epinephrine and the favourable response to BK pathway targeted medications to a similar degree as seen in C1 INH HAE.

The molecular mechanism of C1-INH deficiency is the same in adult and paediatric patients (mutation in SERPING1), resulting in deficient or dysfunctional C1-INH, which leads to elevated levels of the vasodilation signalling peptide BK and subsequently increased vascular permeability and contraction of visceral smooth muscle. Although phenotypic expression may not occur in the first years of life, the same laboratory assessments are used to establish diagnosis (C1-INH functional level and complement component 4 [C4]) in both adult and paediatric patients &gt; 1 year of age. Additionally, across different age groups, no developmental or ontogenic factors have been identified that might influence kallikrein levels. Therefore, the response to berotralstat treatment is expected to be the same in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years as in adult and adolescent subjects aged 12 years and older. Given that the aetiology and pathophysiology of HAE are the same in paediatric and adult patients, it follows that the expected response to treatment is the same in paediatric and adult patients.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Mechanism of action of C1-INH deficiency

Cleavage of HK by plasma kallikrein results in generation of BK. This occurs with activation of contact system proteases (activated factor XII [FXIIa] and plasma kallikrein), which are normally regulated by C1-INH. The Figure 1 below depicts the pathway of BK formation that leads to angioedema. Factor XII (FXII) autoactivates when bound to a negatively charged surface (or endothelial cells) and FXIIa activates prekallikrein to kallikrein. Kallikrein digests HK and releases BK. BK stimulates the endothelial cell B2 receptors and causes vasodilation, which results in fluid leakage to the surrounding tissues. All the reactions leading to BK formation are controlled by C1-INH in plasma.

Figure 1. Plasma BK-forming Pathway

<!-- image -->

Abbreviations: BK = bradykinin; C1-INH = complement 1 esterase inhibitor; FXIIa = activated factor XII; FXII = factor XII; HMW = high molecular weight.

The activation of the BK B2 receptor by BK results in vasodilatation, increased vascular permeability, and smooth muscle contraction, all of which leads to the tissue swelling that characterizes HAE.

## Epidemiology

In several European epidemiology studies, the prevalence of HAE has been reported as between 1: 65,000 and 1: 92,000 and has shown no apparent ethnic variation. The prevalence of HAE in the EEA is estimated based on the worst-case, i.e., the highest prevalence observed in an individual analysis. The highest prevalence was observed in Italy at approximately 1: 65,000. Thus, it can be estimated that HAE affects approximately 0.15 in 10,000 inhabitants of the EU/EEA. Based on a population of 449 million, it can be estimated that there are about 7000 people currently affected with HAE in the EU.

A recent study in Germany estimated HAE prevalence in paediatric patients aged &lt; 12 years as 2.51:100,000. Based on a population of 44 million paediatric individuals aged 2 to &lt; 12 years in the EU, the estimated number of patients aged &lt; 12 years with HAE in the EU is approximately 1100.

The US prevalence of C1-INH-HAE has been estimated by disease experts as 1: 50,000 (Zuraw et al. 2013). Based on a US population of 329 million, the number of patients with HAE would range between approximately 3576 (1: 92,000) and 6580 (1: 50,000). A MAH-commissioned administrative claims database analysis (report H0063.100) concluded that, as of 31 December 2017, the estimated number of patients with HAE in the US was 9376 (95% confidence interval [CI] 9455, 10,017) or approximately 1: 35,000 individuals.

## Clinical presentation

The frequency of attacks in patients not taking a prophylactic medication varies, from rarely in some to every few days in others, with 59% of patients with HAE reporting at least 1 attack per month. Angioedema attacks may or may not be precipitated by a stimulus (such as stress, trauma, infection, or changes in hormonal levels with menses) and typically progress over several hours, with symptoms, if left untreated, subsiding gradually over the following 3 to 5 days. Oropharyngeal and especially laryngeal swelling can be life-threatening, and the progression of symptoms in laryngeal attacks is more rapid than in other locations due to the anatomy of the upper airway.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Mortality risk from asphyxiation is much higher in undiagnosed patients with HAE; however, deaths still occur globally in diagnosed patients with access to care at centres of excellence. Attacks at other sites, including limbs, genitalia, face, and intestines, can be painful, disabling, and disfiguring. Abdominal attacks are especially painful and may result in unnecessary surgical procedures if HAE is not recognized as causal.

HAE has a significant impact on functionality and quality of life (QoL). In a survey of Hospital Anxiety and Depression Scale scores in HAE patients in Spain, Germany, and Denmark, clinically meaningful anxiety and depression were seen in 38% and 14% of patients, respectively, despite the widespread use of modern on-demand treatments for acute attacks. QoL scores on the health-related QoL instrument EuroQoL 5-dimensional, 5-level questionnaire in HAE patients in Sweden show more severe impact on QoL with increased frequency of attacks, and higher absenteeism with increasing attack severity. A US survey of HAE patients reported reduced productivity to a degree similar to severe asthma; missed opportunities in education and career advancement; decreased physical and mental health; and worse depression scores.

## Paediatric clinical presentation

The presentation of disease in paediatric patients with HAE is similar to adults. Both adult and paediatric patients experience recurrent unpredictable acute attacks of painful subcutaneous (SC) or submucosal swelling of the face, larynx, tongue, gastrointestinal tract, limbs, or genitalia which, if untreated, may last up to 5 days and can be fatal. The clinical disease presentation is similar; for example, Nanda et al. reported that in a paediatric cohort of 15 patients, abdominal, peripheral, and laryngeal attacks occurred at least once in 93%, 73%, and 27% of paediatric patients, respectively. A study of 80 adults showed that abdominal, peripheral, and laryngeal attacks (included laryngeal, neck, or oesophagus areas) occurred in 81.5%, 85.2%, and 28.4% of patients, respectively, reflecting a similar profile of disease presentation in paediatric patients and adults.

The frequency and severity of HAE symptoms show significant inter- and intra-individual variation in both adult and paediatric patients, with the frequency of attacks ranging from rarely in some patients to every few days in others.

Laryngeal oedema is less frequent in paediatric patients aged 11 years and younger compared to adults with HAE; however, HAE attacks involving the upper airway are potentially life-threatening regardless of age. Bork et al. have described fatal laryngeal oedema as the initial symptom in a 9year-old male and laryngeal oedema occurring in paediatric patients as young as age 3 years. Nearly half of patients will have an upper airway attack by the age of 18 years.

The age at which attacks begin is variable, with rare reports of initial episodes of angioedema in the perinatal period. Symptoms can start as early as age one to two years. Approximately 40 percent of patients experience their first HAE attack before age 5 years and 75 percent by age 15 years, although repeated attacks in preadolescent children are uncommon. Thus, for the majority of patients, the disease first presents in childhood or adolescence. In a recent long-term survey of children and adolescents with HAE, initial symptoms occurred earlier than that described in the literature, that is, at a mean age of 10 to 12 years and time of the onset of the initial symptoms did not differ between the sexes. Attack frequency usually increases after puberty.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Long-term prophylaxis to minimize the impact of HAE on quality of life is seldom necessary under the age of 6 years, because HAE attacks occur less often at this age. For those who do require preventative therapy, the decision about which of these agents to use, if available, is based on safety, tolerability, and patient preference.

## General Disease Management

The management of HAE involves both the prevention of attacks and treatment of attacks. The International World Allergy Organization/European Academy of Allergy and Clinical Immunology Guideline for the Management of HAE recommends that all patients be evaluated for long-term prophylaxis (LTP) at every visit, have sufficient medication for on-demand treatment of 2 attacks, and carry on-demand treatment at all times.

The long - term prophylaxis of HAE refers to the use of regular medication to reduce the burden of the disease by preventing/attenuating attacks in patients with confirmed HAE. Long-term prophylaxis is given to decrease the number, severity, and length of HAE attacks. Not all patients require it, and the decision to start a patient on long-term prophylaxis should be individualized based on maximizing quality of life. The decision to administer long-term prophylaxis to a specific patient should consider the disease activity, patient's quality of life, availability of health care resources, and failure to achieve adequate control by appropriate on-demand therapy.

## Paediatric Patients Aged 2 to &lt; 12 Years Disease Management

Regardless of age, the management of HAE involves both treatment of acute attacks and consideration of prophylaxis to prevent attacks of HAE. In both of these categories, treatment options are limited for paediatric patients aged &lt; 12 years.

## Long-term prophylaxis, paediatric

Approved therapies for LTP in paediatric patients with HAE are limited to intravenously (IV) or SC administered plasma-derived C1-INHs, including Cinryze (IV infusion for routine prevention against HAE attacks in patients aged 6 years and older and for pre-procedure prevention of angioedema attacks in paediatric patients aged 2 years and older) or Berinert 2000/3000 (Haegarda, administered every 3 to 4 days in paediatric patients aged 8 years and older), as well as a monoclonal antibody inhibitor of plasma kallikrein (Takhzyro [lanadelumab], administered by SC injection every 2 to 4 weeks in patients aged 2 years and older).

Oral androgens (e.g., danazol), have numerous side effects that limit dosing, efficacy, and tolerability; in addition, the safety and effectiveness of danazol in paediatric patients have not been established. The World Allergy Organization/European Academy of Allergy and Clinical Immunology (WAO/EAACI) HAE guidelines do not recommend the use of long- term anabolic androgens for paediatric patients prior to Tanner stage V. Other oral agents used off-label in paediatric patients, such as tranexamic acid, lack efficacy data and are not recommended for prophylaxis per WAO/EAACI guidelines. In 2012, the European Medicines Agency (EMA) Committee for Medicinal Health Products (CHMP) recommended that HAE be deleted as an indication from the tranexamic acid product information due to an unfavourable benefit-risk balance (CHMP 2012). However, in countries where targeted LTP is not available, tranexamic acid may be used for paediatric patients with HAE and preferred to androgens due to its better safety profile.

## Acute management, paediatric

Approved therapies for the treatment of acute attacks in paediatric patients aged &lt; 12 years in the EU include an IV administered plasma-derived C1-INH (Berinert, no lower age limit, studied in age 5 years and older) and a SC administered B2 antagonist (Firazyr), indicated for paediatric patients aged 2 years and older).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.1. Aspects of development

The berotralstat clinical development programme included a first-in-human (FIH) pharmacokinetic (PK) and safety study; clinical pharmacology studies conducted in healthy subjects, subjects with HAE, and subjects with renal or hepatic impairment; and efficacy and safety studies in HAE subjects of daily prophylactic dosing and single doses for acute treatment. The MAH is not pursuing further development of the acute treatment indication.

The basis for the initial marketing application was completion of a controlled study of efficacy and safety in HAE subjects (Part 1 of global Study BCX7353-302 [Study 302] and of Study BCX7353-301 [Study 301] in Japanese subjects) and accumulation of long-term safety data in &gt; 100 HAE subjects completing a 48 week visit in Studies 302 and BCX7353-204 (Study 204) at the marketed dose of 150 mg QD.

Five clinical studies, including Study 304 in paediatric patients, now provide data to support the clinical efficacy and effectiveness of berotralstat in the prevention of HAE attacks. The primary efficacy and effectiveness measurement for these 5 studies was the attack rate experienced by subjects.

Berotralstat granules for oral administration have been developed as an alternative dosage form to allow for dosing flexibility.

This extension of indication application in children and adolescents wih HAE is based on extrapolation using PK and safety data to match effective and safe exposures betwenn the paediatric and the adult (reference) population.

## Adults and Adolescent Patients Aged 12 Years and Older

The clinical development program for berotralstat for the indication of prevention of attacks in patients with HAE in adults and adolescent patients aged 12 years and older is comprised of 2 Phase 3 studies (Study 302, pivotal study, and 301), and 2 Phase 2 studies (Studies 203 and 204). The Phase 1 clinical pharmacology program in support of the prevention indication comprises 14 completed studies.

The Phase 1 studies conducted to support the development of berotralstat for the prophylactic indication include the following, all of which are completed with clinical study reports (CSRs) available:

- o FIH single- and multiple-ascending dose, including a Japanese ethnobridging component (Study BCX7353 101)
- o Absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (Study BCX7353 104)
- o Drug-drug interaction (Studies BCX7353 102, BCX7353 105, BCX7353 111, BCX7353 112, BCX7353 115, and BCX7353 119)
- o Bioavailability of different dosage forms (Study BCX7353 103)
- o Thorough QT (Study BCX7353 106)
- o Bioequivalence of berotralstat capsules (Study BCX7353 113)
- o Renal impairment (Study BCX7353 107)
- o Hepatic impairment (Study BCX7353 108)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Studies in support of the acute treatment indication include the following:

- o Phase 1 single-dose PK and pharmacodynamics (PD) study in subjects with HAE (Study BCX7353 109) Note: results from this study are reported in Section 2.1.3.2 of m2.7.2, as this study provided PK and PD data in subjects with HAE, and therefore the study is considered part of the Phase 1 clinical pharmacology program.
- o Phase 1 bioavailability study (Study BCX7353 114). Part 2 evaluated the relative bioavailability of the berotralstat commercial capsule formulation and granules formulation.
- o Phase 2, randomized, double-blind, dose -ranging study (Study BCX7353 202 [Study 202])
- Phase 2 and 3 studies in support of the prophylactic indication in subjects with HAE include the following:
- o Phase 2, 28 day, randomized double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-ranging study (Study BCX7353 203 [Study 203]) that established proof of concept
- o Phase 2, long-term, open-label safety study (Study 204)
- o Phase 3, double blind, placebo-controlled randomized studies (Studies 302 and 301) to confirm efficacy and safety of berotralstat.

## Paediatric Patients Aged 2 to &lt; 12 Years

The clinical development program for berotralstat for the indication of prevention of attacks in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years with HAE is supported by BCX7353-304 (Study 304) and part 2 of Study BCX7353 114.

The MAH did not seek Scientific Advice from the CHMP in relation to the current application.

## 2.2. Description of the product

Berotralstat is a potent, small-molecule inhibitor of plasma kallikrein that has been developed for the prevention of attacks in patients with HAE.

## Figure 2. Structural Formula

<!-- image -->

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Berotralstat (BCX7353) is ((+)-1-(3-(aminomethyl)phenyl)-N-(5-((3cyanophenyl)((cyclopropylmethyl) amino)methyl)-2-fluorophenyl)-3-(trifluoromethyl)-1Hpyrazole-5carboxamide) dihydrochloride.

Orladeyo (berotralstat; BCX7353) is approved in a hard capsule formulation for routine prevention of recurrent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in adult and adolescent patients aged 12 years and older.

## 3. Quality aspects

## Introduction

This line extension aims to add film-coated granules in sachet as a new pharmaceutical form to the already authorished capsules associated with new strengths.

The finished product is presented as film-coated granules in sachet, each sachet containing 72 mg, 96 mg, 108 mg or 132 mg of berotralstat (as dihydrochloride) as active substance.

Other ingredients are:

- Granules core: crospovidone (type A), magnesium stearate, silica, colloidal anhydrous and starch, pregelatinised;
- Granules coating: butylated methacrylate copolymer, basic (E 1205), sodium laurilsulphate, silica, colloidal anhydrous, stearic acid, talc (E 553b) and titanium dioxide (E 171).

The product is available in PET/AL/PE foil laminate sachets as described in section 6.5 of the SmPC.

## 3.1. Active substance

No new documentation has been submitted about the active substance. This is acceptable as the proposed pharmaceutical form (i.e. film-coated granules) contains the same active substance form the same source as the commercially available pharmaceutical form (capsules) and no additional controls on functionality related characteristics are proposed for the new pharmaceutical form.

## 3.2. Finished medicinal product

## 3.2.1. Description of the product and pharmaceutical development

## Description of the product

The finished product is presented as white to off-white, plain-faced, round film-coated granules, approximately 2 mm in diameter.

## Pharmaceutical development

The film-coated granules were developed as a solid oral dosage form that allows for dosing flexibility for paediatric patients 2 years of age and older. The quality target product profile was assessed for this granule formulation compared to the approved capsule formulation. The formulation of the granules consists of the same blend (active substance and excipients) in the same proportions as the approved capsule formulation. Appropriate data from development of the capsules was leveraged in the development of the granules.

Finished product batches with varying active substance particle size were examined, and no impact on content uniformity or dissolution was observed. The selected excipients are commonly used in medicinal product manufacture. A comprehensive discussion on the Functionality Related Characteristics (FRCs) of all excipients has been provided.

Compatibility of the active substance with the coating material has been appropriately demonstrated. Compatibility of the active substance with the the core granule excipients has been demonstrated during the development of berotralstat capsules and reference to these studies is considered acceptable. The stability data presented further supports compatibility of ingredients within the formulation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

All excipients are well known pharmaceutical ingredients, and their quality is compliant with Ph. Eur. standards. There are no novel excipients used in the finished product formulation. The list of excipients is included in section 6.1 of the SmPC.

During clinical studies conducted as part of the development of berotralstat capsules, it was observed and reported that the active substance had a bitter taste. Therefore, the inclusion of a commercially available film-coat with pH dependent solubility was examined during the development. It has been shown that the coating remains practically insoluble in the mouth (pH ~ 6.8) and provides taste-masking, while dissolving in the stomach (pH ~ 1.2) and thus allowing for immediate release of berotralstat. Therefore, a coating weight gain of 10 % w/w was selected to provide complete granule coverage. The suitability of the coating in the granules was supported by an accelerated stability study.

Differences between formulations of research and development, clinical and registration batches were stated, and comparable dissolution profiles were obtained.

The dissolution method has been developed based on the commercial method used for the capsules. The active substance is highly soluble in the dissolution media. The CHMP considered as major objection (MO1) that the information presented on the development of the dissolution method was not sufficient as the agitation speed in the proposed QC dissolution method used was not sufficiently justified, the discriminatory nature of the proposed QC dissolution method was not appropriately demonstrated, and the suitability of the dissolution method for the highest strength (132 mg) was not demonstrated. As response to this MO1, the dissolution method was optimised based on the approved capsule dissolution method. The choice of dissolution apparatus and media was suitably justified. Moreover, the agitation speed used results in a dissolution method with acceptable discriminatory power considering the active substance is a BCS class 1 substance with high solubility in the dissolution medium. Dissolution results for three batches of the 132 mg strength generated using the revised QC method were provided. These data demonstrate the dissolution performance of the highest strength under the currently proposed method conditions; the suitability of the QC dissolution method for the 132 mg strength has been sufficiently demonstrated. Therefore, the MO was considered resolved.

Compatibility studies were conducted to support the administration of granules to paediatric patients. Non-acidic soft food vehicles are recommended to ensure the integrity of the coating for taste-masking purposes in addition to the recommendation that all granules are to be consumed within 10 minutes of being sprinkled over food (SmPC section 4.2). The compatible foods listed in SmPC (section 4.2) have been adequately justified by the data provided.

## Manufacturing process development

The manufacturing process was based on the manufacture of the granule in the capsule, with additional compression and coating steps included and process parameters examined. Design of experiment studies were performed; no critical process parameters were identified. No designed space was applied by the MAH.

## 3.2.2. Manufacture of the product and process controls

The finished product is manufactured at one manufacturing site.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Satisfactory evidence of GMP compliance has been provided for all sites involved in the manufacturing, testing and batch release of the finished product.

The manufacturing process consists of 6 main steps: intra-granular blending, dry granulation, extragranular blending, compression, coating, and filling into unit dose sachets. The process is considered to be a standard manufacturing process.

The active substance dispensing was identified as the critical process step, with all other steps controlled by the process parameters set during development and supported by acceptable studies performed, e.g. blend uniformity.

Major steps of the manufacturing process have been validated by a number of studies. It has been demonstrated that the manufacturing process is capable of producing the finished product of intended quality in a reproducible manner. The in-process controls are adequate for this pharmaceutical form.

## 3.2.3. Control of Finished Product

The finished product release and shelf-life specifications include appropriate tests for this kind of dosage form: description (visual), identification (HPLC (RT), HPLC (UV)), assay (HPLC), degradation products (HPLC), uniformity of dosage units (Ph. Eur.), dissolution (Ph. Eur.), water content (KF), Nnitroso-berotralstat (LC-MS/MS), and microbial limits testing (Ph. Eur.).

The finished product specification has been set considering relevant Ph. Eur. chapters including the granule's dosage form monograph and ICH guidance documents.

The total degradant release data from the batch analysis for batches of the finished product are provided. The data demonstrates that the total degradants of the finished product are well controlled within the limits of the proposed specification, NMT 1.0%.

During evaluation, the proposed dissolution limit was not acceptable based on the data provided and the CHMP raised a MO (MO2) requesting to tighten dissolution limit. The applicant, as a response, proposed to tighten the dissolution limit. Dissolution data was provided for the 132 mg strength using the updated method. The proposed dissolution limit was also not acceptable as the dose of 132 mg was not used in a clinical study and therefore the dissolution specification limit should be set based on the performance of relevant batches tested using the same method intended for routine QC. The MAH as a response agreed to further tighten further the specification and the MO was considered resolved.

The potential presence of elemental impurities in the finished product has been assessed following a risk-based approach in line with the ICH Q3D Guideline for Elemental Impurities. Batch analysis data on 3 batches using a validated ICP-MS method was provided, demonstrating that each relevant elemental impurity was not detected above 30% of the respective PDE. Based on the risk assessment and the presented batch data it can be concluded that it is not necessary to include any elemental impurity controls.

A risk assessment concerning the potential presence of nitrosamine impurities in the finished product has been performed, during evaluation the CHMP requested as a MO (MO3) to confirm the absence of all nitrosamines in the finished product batches by analysis with appropriate methods. It was further requested that finished product batches should be tested for the stated N -nitroso species arising from active substance manufacture as well as small molecule nitrosamines that may form due to the presence of nitrosating agents in the active substance manufacturing process. Based on any nitrosamine levels that may be found to be present, the applicant was also asked to fully justify why there is no risk of nitrosamines levels reaching or exceeding limits outlined in the EMA Nitrosamines Q&amp;A and associated Appendix 1 by end of the proposed shelf-life, through further testing on stability or appropriate justification. As a response, the nitrosamine risk assessment has been updated with sufficient detail in relation to the three additional active substance derived nitrosamines identified arising from active substance manufacture. The justification provided for the control of these nitrosamines at release, in addition to small molecule nitrosamines N ￼ nitrosodiethylamine (NDEA) and N ￼ nitrosodimethylamine (NDMA), is acceptable. Moreover, the substance specific nitrosamine N - nitrosoberotralstat is appropriately controlled in the finished product specification. The specification limits correspond to an acceptable AI limit of 1500 ng/day which has been accepted by Nitrosamines Safety Operational Expert Group (NSOEG). Based on the information provided, it is accepted that there is no risk of nitrosamine impurities in the active substance or the related finished product and the MO is resolved.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Analytical procedures and reference standards

The analytical methods used have been adequately described and appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards used for testing has been presented.

## Batch analysis

Batch analysis results are provided for 3 commercial scale batches per strength confirming the consistency of the manufacturing process and its ability to manufacture to the intended product specification.

## Container closure system

The primary packaging is PET/AL/PE foil laminate sachets. The material complies with Ph. Eur. and EC requirements. The choice of the container closure system has been validated by stability data and is adequate for the intended use of the product.

## 3.2.4. Stability of the product

The shelf life of 2 years without any special storage conditions as stated in the SmPC (section 6.3) are acceptable in the proposed container as stated in the SmPC, is justified by the available stability data.

Real time stability data from three commercial batches of 66 mg and 150 mg of finished product stored for up to 24 months under long term conditions (25 ºC / 60% RH) and for up to 6 months under accelerated conditions (40 ºC / 75% RH) according to the ICH guidelines were provided. The batches are identical to those proposed for marketing and were packed in the primary packaging proposed for marketing.

A bracketing design was used to demonstrate the stability of the proposed commercial strengths in alignment with ICH Q1D. The proposed commercial strengths, 72 mg, 96 mg, 108 mg, and 132 mg are bracketed by the registration batch strengths of 66 mg and 150 mg. This is acceptable because for each product strength, the same granules are filled into the same container closure resulting only in a variation in the fill count across all product strengths and all dose strengths originate from the same bulk batch of granules.

Samples were tested for appearance, assay, unspecified degradant products, dissolution water content, and microbial limits. The analytical procedures used are stability indicating.

All batches met specifications at long term conditions; however, the accelerated study showed a minor increase in individual and total impurities at 6 months, with all results remaining within specification.

Six months of accelerated stability data for N -nitroso-berotralstat has been provided for four batches of finished product. Although an increase in nitrosamine content is observed, the trend is not significant as each batch complies with the specification limit throughout the 6-month study. Based on the accelerated stability data provided, no temperature storage conditions are required for the finished product.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

At the time of the assessment, the proposed shelf life could not be accepted as the finished product specification was not acceptable in light of the objections raised in relation to the control of nitrosamines and the dissolution test/limits. Therefore, the CHMP raised a MO (MO4) regarding the proposed shelf-life of the product. As the two issues have been solved the proposed shelf-life was eventually accepted and the MO resolved.

The following stress conditions were examined with testing performed as per the HPLC assay and related substances method: 0.15 % hydrogen peroxide for 1 hour, 0.1 N hydrochloric acid at 60 °C for one day, 1N sodium hydroxide for 5 days, 100 °C for 5 days. The method was deemed to be stabilityindicating by confirmation of peak purity; the finished product was shown to be sensitive to basic conditions.

A photostability study in one batch was conducted as per ICH Q1B. No significant changes were observed. A change in degradation products as well as a slowing of the dissolution profile was observed in the direct exposure granules. It was concluded that the primary packaging configuration provides sufficient protection from light.

An open dish humidity study was performed in one batch of bulk finished product. Study samples were exposed to 40 °C/ 75% RH conditions for 14 days. Chemical and physical attributes and dissolution profiles were assessed at the start and end of the study. No differences were observed in the critical quality attributes.

A bulk hold study was conducted to assess extended hold times, up to 12 months, of the finished product in the bulk packaging configuration. The bulk packaged granules were stored under warehouse conditions (18.3 °C - 25.0 °C/ 30% - 60% RH). Dissolution testing was conducted. Results at each time point were compared to the initial results. There was no significant change in the critical quality attributes.

The stability data justify the proposed shelf-life of 2 years without any special storage conditions in the proposed container as stated in the SmPC (section 6.3) are acceptable.

## 3.2.5. Adventitious agents

No excipients derived from animal or human origin have been used.

## 3.3. Discussion and conclusions on quality aspects

Information on development, manufacture and control of the finished product has been presented in a satisfactory manner.

Four major objections were raised during the procedure concerning: (1) the dissolution method development, (2) the proposed dissolution limit, (3) the nitrosamines risk assessment, and (4) the shelf life of the finished product. In response to MO1, the applicant provided dissolution results for three batches of the highest strength generated using the revised QC dissolution method. These data demonstrated the dissolution performance of the highest strength under the currently proposed method conditions. Therefore, the suitability of the revised QC dissolution method was demonstrated. It was agreed to tighten the dissolution limit as requested to address MO2. As a response to address MO3 in relation to nitrosamines, the nitrosamine risk assessment has been updated with sufficient detail, justification for the control of these nitrosamines at release has been provided, and substance specific nitrosamine N -nitrosoberotralstat is appropriately controlled in the finished product specification. Regarding MO4, as MO2 and MO3 were resolved, the proposed shelf life is acceptable. All four MOs are thus considered resolved.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The results of tests carried out indicate consistency and uniformity of important product quality characteristics, and these in turn lead to the conclusion that the product should have a satisfactory and uniform performance in clinical use.

## 3.4. Recommendation(s) for future quality development

Not applicable

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4. Non-clinical aspects

## Introduction

This group line extension with an extension of indication is supported by a non-clinical package as follows: A GLP enhanced Ames test on N-nitroso-berotralstat (BCX21934), the berotralstat nitrosamine drug substance-related impurity (NDSRI) (study 20494410) and two nonclinical toxicity studies in juvenile rats (a non-GLP 7-day tolerability and 28 ￼ day dose-range-finding study (study 9001319) and a GLP, definitive 7-week study with a 4 week recovery period (study 806-333)).

## 4.1. Toxicology

## 4.1.1. Genotoxicity

The ability of N-nitroso-berotralstat (BCX21934) to induce reverse mutations in five bacterial strains (TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537 and WP2 uvrA (pKM101)) in the presence and absence of rat and hamster S9 metabolic activation was evaluated using an enhanced Ames assay test.

When testing N-nitroso-berotralstat dissolved in DMSO at dose levels ranging from 1.58 to 5000 µg/plate, revertant colony counts across five bacterial strains were similar or lower than vehicle control values in the presence or absence of rat and hamster S9 metabolic activation. N-nitroso-berotralstat was not considered mutagenic due to negative results in the enhanced Ames assay test under the conditions and according to the criteria of the test protocol. However, several issues have been noted regarding the assay acceptance criteria in terms of the selection of nitrosamine positive controls, dissolution and results of nitrosamine positive controls, and solvent concentration in the preincubation mixture.

The applicant conducted a second enhanced Ames Test for N-Nitrosamines in accordance with the most recent guidelines and specific recommendations received from the Nitrosamines Safety Operational Expert Group (NS OEG) through a type IB procedure concluded in December 2025. Based on the negative results from the second enhanced Ames test, a 1500 ng/day limit for N-nitroso-berotralstat (BCX21934) has been accepted by NSOEG /CHMP as part of the Step 2 nitrosamine reporting notification.

## 4.1.2. Other toxicity studies

## Juvenile rat toxicity

A non-GLP 7-day tolerability and 28-day dose range-finding studies in juvenile rats (Study 9001319) were conducted in order to establish a maximum tolerated dose (MTD) and provide information for selection of dosages to be used in a subsequent, definitive, GLP-compliant 7-week toxicity study of berotralstat in juvenile rats with a 4-week recovery period (Study 806-333).

In an initial tolerability phase, berotralstat was administered once daily (QD) by oral gavage from PND 21 to 27 at doses 0, 10, 30 and 100 mg/kg. The high dose of 100 mg/kg was associated with reduced body weight gain, body weights and food consumption in both male and female animals, indicating a low tolerability.

In a dose range-finding (DRF) phase, berotralstat was administered QD by oral gavage from PND 21 to 48 at doses 0, 5, 20 and 50 mg/kg. Berotralstat was generally well tolerated up to 50 mg/kg, with no mortality or clinical signs and no significant changes in body weights, food consumption, clinical chemistry parameters, organ weights or macroscopic findings. In female animals at all dose levels and in male animals dosed with ≥20 mg/kg, minimal to mild biliary epithelial hypertrophy with cytoplasmic vacuolation was observed microscopically in the liver. Additionally, minimal macrophage vacuolation was also noted in the portal area, jejunum, ileum and mesenteric lymph node at dose levels ≥20 mg/kg. However, in the absence of correlating clinical pathology parameters and functional impairment, these microscopic findings were not considered adverse by the applicant. The NOAEL for the DRF phase was considered to be 50 mg/kg/day, corresponding to the mean Cmax and AUC values of 570 ng/mL and 6840 ng·h/mL for males and 896 ng/mL and 11800 hr·ng/mL for females, respectively, on PND 48.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In a definitive GLP-compliant 7-week juvenile toxicity study with a 4-week recovery period, berotralstat was administered QD by oral gavage from PND 21 to 69 at doses 0, 5, 20 and 50 mg/kg to support the use of berotralstat in patients aged 2 years and older.

No berotralstat-related effects were observed on survival, clinical findings, body weights/body weight gain, sexual maturation, femur/tibia length and width measurements, hematology parameters, or macroscopic findings at any dose levels.

Berotralstat-related effects on clinical pathology parameters were observed at the 50 mg/kg/day dose (increase in cholesterol concentrations and decrease sodium concentrations in females, decrease in urine pH in males, increased liver weights in females). They were resolved following a 4-week recovery. At the end of the treatment, berotralstat-related findings were seen in the liver, adrenal gland (females only), mandibular and mesenteric lymph nodes, ileum, jejunum and spleen. In the liver, hypertrophy and cytoplasmic vacuolation of the biliary epithelial cells were observed in males and females at dose levels ≥ 20 mg/kg/day. The severity of the findings was greater in the females with increased liver weights at 50 mg/kg/day, while slightly elevated AST and ALT values were noted in both sexes at the same dose. Despite clear evidence of dose-dependent effect of berotralstat on the liver, the reversibility of all these parameters and endpoints was demonstrated at the end of the recovery period. Additionally, vacuolation was seen in the cortex of the adrenal glands (females at 50 mg/kg/day), macrophages in the sinus of the mandibular lymph node (males and females at 50 mg/kg/day), macrophages in the sinus of the mesenteric lymph node (males at 50 mg/kg/day and females at ≥ 20 mg/kg/day ), macrophages in the lamina propria of the ileum and/or jejunum (males and females at 50 mg/kg/day), and macrophages in the white pulp of the spleen (males and females at 50 mg/kg/day).

The NOAEL for both male and female rats was established at 50 mg/kg, the highest dose level evaluated in the definitive juvenile toxicity study. On PND 69, combined-sex Cmax and AUC0-24 values were 1170 ng/mL and 16,800 ng.hr/mL, respectively. The NOAEL AUC0-24 is 6.5-, 5.9-, 6.1-, and 5.8fold higher than the simulated AUC0-24 exposures for paediatric dosing at the recommended doses of BCX7353 based on body weight at 72 mg (15 to &lt; 24 kg), 96 mg (24 to &lt; 32 kg), 108 mg (32 to &lt; 40 kg), and 132 mg (≥ 40 kg) QD.

TK data showed that systemic exposures were generally comparable in both genders on PND 21 and PND 69 (F:M AUC0-24 ratios ranged from 1.05 to 1.45 at dose levels ≥ 20 mg/kg). Following once daily oral administration, Cmax and AUC0-24 values increased slightly more than dose-proportional on PND 21 and PND 69, while accumulation ratios of 5.85, 3.26 and 2.99 were measured at 5, 20 and 50 mg/kg, respectively.

## 4.1.3. Ecotoxicity/environmental risk assessment

An updated environmental risk assessment (ERA) was performed to include the proposed extension in accordance with the Guideline on the environmental risk assessment of medicinal products for human use (EMEA/CHMP/SWP/4440/00 Rev.1-Corr*). It is considered that prevalence used in refinement of PEC in the initial MAA is relevant for this procedure. Thus, the phase I PECsw of berotralstat remains below the action limit of 0.01 µg/L and no further risk assessment (Phase II, Tier A) is required.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The experimentally derived Log P values for neutral and cationic berotralstat 2HCl were 4.19 and 2.15, respectively. The Log D values were -1.56, 0.28, 2.35, and 3.99 at pH 3, 5, 7, and 9, respectively. These values are below the trigger value of 4.5. Therefore, berotralstat is not a PBT substance.

## 4.2. Overall discussion and conclusions on non-clinical aspects

## 4.2.1. Discussion

The applicant has submitted a GLP Enhanced Ames test on N-nitroso-berotralstat and two nonclinical toxicity studies in juvenile rats to support this grouped line extension and variation application.

## Toxicology

The initially submitted Enhanced Ames test for N-nitroso-berotralstat (BCX21934) was not adequate to allow an acceptable intake (AI) limit of 1500 ng/day. The applicant conducted a second Enhanced Ames test for N-Nitrosamines in accordance with the most recent guidelines and specific recommendations received from NS OEG. Based on the negative results from the second enhanced Ames test, the 1500 ng/day limit for N-nitroso-berotralstat (BCX21934) has been accepted by NS OEG as part of the Step 2 nitrosamine reporting notification. The final report for the second enhanced Ames test was submitted with a Type IB variation on 02 December 2025 and received a favourable opinion on 07 January 2026 (EMADOC-1700519818-2798972).

To support the use of berotralstat in patients aged 2 years and older, a definitive GLP-compliant 7week toxicity study in juvenile rats with a 4-week recovery period was conducted. Animals were once daily (QD) administered by oral gavage from PND 21 to PND 69, thereby covering the age in humans from 2 up to 12 years old. Berotralstat-related effects on clinical pathology parameters were observed at the 50 mg/kg/day dose. The applicant considered the findings non-adverse as they resolved following a 4-week recovery period. At the end of the treatment, berotralstat-related findings were seen in the liver, adrenal gland (females only), mandibular and mesenteric lymph nodes, ileum, jejunum and spleen. The severity of the findings was greater in the females with increased liver weights at 50 mg/kg/day. Additionally, vacuolation was seen in the cortex of the adrenal glands, macrophages in the sinus of the mandibular lymph node, macrophages in the sinus of the mesenteric lymph node, macrophages in the lamina propria of the ileum and/or jejunum, and macrophages in the white pulp of the spleen. The applicant stated that vacuolation of biliary epithelial cells, adrenal cortex cells, and macrophages was suggestive of phospholipidosis. Similar liver findings were observed in sub-chronic and chronic toxicity studies in adult rats, in which phospholipidosis was confirmed by electron microscopy. The severity of biliary epithelial cell hypertrophy and vacuolation was increased in males and females with increased liver weights in the females administered 50 mg/kg/day. These findings were considered non-adverse due to their reversibility and the absence of clinical chemistry data indicative of biliary damage. There was no evidence of secondary functional changes in the affected organs or animals as demonstrated by the absence of severe clinical signs, lack of alterations in food consumption or body weights, and absence of any relevant clinical pathology.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The NOAEL for both male and female rats was considered to be 50 mg/kg, the highest dose level evaluated in the definitive juvenile toxicity study. On PND 69, combined-sex Cmax and AUC0-24 values were 1170 ng/mL and 16,800 ng.hr/mL, respectively. The NOAEL AUC0-24 is 6.5-, 5.9-, 6.1-, and 5.8fold higher than the simulated AUC0-24 exposures for paediatric dosing at the recommended doses of BCX7353 based on body weight at 72 mg (15 to &lt; 24 kg), 96 mg (24 to &lt; 32 kg), 108 mg (32 to &lt; 40 kg), and 132 mg (≥ 40 kg) QD. These exposure multiples in juvenile rats support the use of berotralstat for routine prevention of recurrent HAE attacks in children aged 2 to less than 12 years from a non-clinical point of view.

## Environmental Risk Assessment (ERA)

The applicant has provided an updated ERA in accordance with the Guideline on the environmental risk assessment of medicinal products for human use (EMEA/CHMP/SWP/4440/00 Rev.1-Corr*). It is agreed that based on the updated PEC figure no further risk assessment (Phase II, Tier A) is required.

## 4.2.2. Conclusions

The submitted non-clinical data are supportive of the extension of indication and are considered acceptable. The juvenile animal toxicity findings have been adequately reflected in the SmPC section 5.3.

## 5.2.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 5.2.2.1. Introduction

To support this application, the MAH has provided pharmacokinetic (PK) data from a single relative bioavailability study, Study BCX7353-114, and a Phase 3 study in paediatric patients with HAE, Study BCX7353-304. The PK data from these studies was used to develop a population PK (popPK) model for paediatrics patients with HAE aged 2 to &lt;12 years, and simulations based on this model were used to support dosing recommendations in the paediatric patient group.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.2.2. Evaluation and qualification of models

## 5.2.2.2.1. Population Pharmacokinetics

Two population pharmacokinetic models were submitted to support this application:

- OLA-MS-001: Berotralstat dose selection for paediatric patients with hereditary angioedema
- BCX7353-MS-002: Population Pharmacokinetic Modeling and Simulation of Berotralstat Exposures for paediatric Participants with Hereditary Angioedema Who Are 2 to &lt; 12 Years of Age

## OLA-MS-001

This report summarised the pharmacokinetic (PK) and pharmacodynamic (PD) rationale for dose selection in paediatric patients between 2-12 years of age and was used to inform the doses investigated in Study BCX7353-304.

A three-compartment model with first-order absorption and linear elimination was derived from 12 studies in 753 subjects. The 12 studies included 9 Phase 1 studies, two Phase 2 studies, and the pivotal Phase 3 study supporting FDA and EMA approval in adult and adolescent patients (BCX7353302). The model was simplified to focus on scaling to paediatrics. The only covariates included in the revised model were weight on clearance, weight on volume, and dose on bioavailability. The estimation used the SAEM algorithm in NONMEM version 7.4.3.

## BCX7353-MS-002

The objectives of this popPK analysis were to:

- characterise the PK of berotralstat in paediatric patients 2 to &lt; 12 years of age with HAE, and
- support berotralstat dose recommendation in paediatric patients 2 to &lt; 12 years of age with HAE.

A total of 11 studies with PK sampling met the predefined criteria for inclusion in the PopPK dataset. There were six Phase 1 studies (Studies BCX7353-101, -103, -106, -113, -114, and -116 in healthy adults), two Phase 2 studies (Studies BCX7353-203, - 204 in adult and adolescents ≥12 years with HAE), and three Phase 3 studies (Studies BCX7353-301, and - 302 in adult and adolescents ≥12 years with HAE, and Study BCX7353-304 paediatric participants aged 2 to &lt;12 years with HAE). The source PopPK dataset included 14292 PK samples collected from 866 participants. 1110 records were excluded from the analysis based on prespecified inclusion/exclusion criteria. Of the remaining 13182 PK samples, 788 (6%) were below the assay quantitation limit and were flagged for exclusion from the analysis. The resultant PopPK dataset included 12394 PK samples collected from 786 study participants.

A schematic of the final PopPK model is shown in Figure 3. The final popPK model was a twocompartment model with dual (fast and slow) lagged parallel first-order absorption processes and linear elimination. Both absorption rate (characterized by Ka1) and extent of absorption (characterized by Frel) were dose- and formulation-dependent. In addition, a reduction in CL/F after the first dose of QD regimens was included in the model to describe higher steady-state exposures than expected based on Day 1 CL/F. Apparent clearance and volume terms were scaled by body weight. Interindividual variability was estimated on CL/F, Vc/F, Q/F, Vp/F, and Ka, and IOV was estimated for CL/F and Vc/F, coinciding with the onset of reduced CL/F. Parameter estimates for the final PopPK model are shown in Table 6.

The clinical importance of the covariates in the final PopPK model were assessed by examining the impact of each covariate on the following berotralstat exposures: area under the plasma concentration-time curve (AUC) over the 24-hour dosing interval at steady state (AUC0-24,ss), maximum plasma concentrations at steady state (Cmax,ss), average plasma concentration at steady state (Cave,ss), and trough plasma concentrations at steady state (Ctrough,ss).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The impact of statistically significant covariates in the final PopPK model on berotralstat Ctrough,ss, Cmax,ss and AUC0-24,ss was illustrated with forest plots (Figure 7, Figure 8, and Figure 9). Dose and formulation were significant covariates on absorption parameters (Frel, Ka1, and absorption lag time for rapid absorption compartment [Tlag1]) but had negligible impact on berotralstat exposures, while higher Frel in the paediatric population impacted exposures by up to 20%. The forest plots showed that body weight had a clinically relevant influence on exposure and that lower body weight (≤ 40 kg) was the primary factor contributing to higher exposures in the paediatric population.

## 5.2.2.2.2. Physiology based pharmacokinetic model

No physiology based pharmacokinetic models were submitted to support this application.

## 5.2.2.3. Absorption

To support this grouped variation to include several strengths of the new dosage form of berotralstat granules, the MAH has submitted Study BCX7353-114 (Study 114) to assess the relative bioavailability of the granules formulation with the capsule formulation.

## Study BCX7353-114

Part 2 of this study was a multiple-dose, open-label, randomized, 3-period evaluation of the safety, tolerability, and relative bioavailability of 2 formulations of berotralstat: the commercial capsule formulation and the mini-tablet (granules) formulation tested in Part 1. In Periods 1 and 2, subjects received multiple once-daily doses of 150 mg berotralstat in capsule formulation and multiple oncedaily doses of 150 mg berotralstat as mini-tablets in randomized sequence. Following completion of Periods 1 and 2, all subjects received multiple once-daily doses of 300 mg berotralstat as mini-tablets in Period 3.

The relevant Cmax and AUCtau ratios for Regimens D, E, and F for study Days 1 and 7 are shown in Table 8. For the Regimen E/Regimen D comparison on Day 1, the AUCtau (90% CI) fell within the FDAspecified bioequivalence range of 0.8 to 1.25 but the lower bound of the 90% CI for Cmax fell below 80% therefore not achieving bioequivalence. For the Regimen E/Regimen D comparison on Day 7, both ratios and their 90% CIs fell within the specified range thus demonstrating bioequivalence between the capsule and mini-tablet formulations. On the basis of the Day 7 evaluation, bioequivalence was established between the capsule and mini-tablet formulations at a dose of 150 mg.

For the Regimen F/Regimen D comparison on both Days 1 and 7, the dose-normalized ratios and their 90% CIs were &gt; 100% indicating berotralstat exposures greater than dose-proportional. In addition, the upper bounds of the 90% CIs were greater than the FDA-specified limit of 125% for the Regimen F/Regimen D comparisons on both Days 1 and 7; therefore, bioequivalence with the capsule formulation was not demonstrated at the 300 mg dose level.

## 5.2.2.4. Distribution

Berotralstat showed a high apparent volume of distribution (Vd/F) in the analysis of Study BCX7353304 using the final popPK model. The GM (CV%) for the Vss/F was 3122 L (40%) for all paediatric cohorts. This is similar to the Vd/F seen in adults in the initial MAA, in Study 106 where GM (CV%) Vd/F was 3123 L (40%). This suggests that distribution PK is similar between the paediatric and adult population.

## 5.2.2.5. Metabolism

No new studies investigating metabolism in paediatrics were included in this application.

Ontogeny of cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes and renal maturation are factors that must be considered when extrapolating to paediatric subpopulations. However, neither of these factors are considered relevant for berotralstat. Berotralstat is metabolized by CYP2D6 and CYP3A4, and renally excreted (&lt;10%). As CYP2D6/CYP3A4 activities and renal function reach adult levels by 1 to 2 years of age it is unlikely that age-related changes in metabolism and renal function will play a significant role in determining the PK profile of berotralstat in the proposed study paediatric population (age 2 to &lt; 12 years).

## 5.2.2.6. Elimination

No new studies investigating elimination were included in this application.

Based on the model, CL/F decreased with decreasing body weight. For paediatric patients weighing 20, 40, and 50 kg (corresponding to 50, 30, and 20 kg decreases in body weight relative to a typical 70 kg adult), CL/F is expected to decrease by 52%, 28%, and 18%, respectively.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.2.7. Dose proportionality and time dependency

No new studies with an objective to demonstrate dose proportionality were performed for this application. In the initial MAA increase in exposure (Cmax and AUC) were shown to be more than dose proportional.

In Part 2 of Study BCX7353-114, a more than dose proportional increase in the Cmax and AUCtau was demonstrated between Regimen E (150 mg berotralstat granules) and Regimen F (300 mg berotralstat granules). This indicates that the more than dose proportional increase in exposure also applies to the granules formulation.

## 5.2.2.8. Pharmacokinetics in the target population

Pharmacokinetics in the target population of paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt;12 years old with HAE was evaluated by Study BCX7353-304 and by pharmacometrics analysis in reports BCX7353-MS-002 and BCX7353-MS-003 described below.

## Study BCX7353-304

The primary objective of this study was to describe the PK parameters of berotralstat administered orally to paediatric participants with HAE aged 2 to &lt;12 years and weighing ≥ 12 kg, with the primary endpoint being the characterisation of the PK profile of berotralstat in participants aged 2 to &lt;12 years.

The berotralstat doses for this study were determined by extrapolation based upon an existing popPK model in adult healthy volunteers and HAE patients ≥ 12 years, existing pharmacokinetic - pharmacodynamic models of kallikrein inhibition in adult healthy volunteers and in HAE patients ≥ 12 years, and correlations between kallikrein inhibition and attack r eduction in HAE patients ≥ 12 years (OLA-MS-001).

The treatments for Study 304 are in line with the doses identified by the simulations performed in OLA-MS-001 and are outlined in Table 9 below.

## 5.2.2.9. Special populations

Data on special populations were not included in this application, except for paediatric patients with HAE who are considered the target population for this application.

## 5.2.2.10. Pharmacokinetic interaction studies

No pharmacokinetic interactions studies were submitted as part of this application.

## 5.2.2.11. Exposure relevant for safety evaluation

In the initial MAA, in the exposure-response analysis, there was a statistically significant positive relationship between ΔΔQTcF and berotralstat concentration (p&lt;0.001). It is stated in the Summary of Clinical Pharmacology that a concentration of 222 ng/mL is estimated to provide a ΔΔQTcF 2 -sided 90% UB of 10 msec, the QT prolongation threshold of regulatory concern outlined in ICH E14.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 10 provides a comparison of the simulated paediatric Ctrough,ss and Cmax,ss for paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt;12 years compared with the reference ranges for adult subjects based on the final model with estimated exponents. The simulated data has been summarised for the median, 5 th  and 9 th percentiles in 2 kg width bins for closer inspection of the data. The median Ctrough,ss for all paediatric weight ranges match the adult reference data closely. There is an increase in median Cmax,ss with decreasing body weight but the median Cmax,ss remained below the 222 ng/mL limit for QT prolongation for all body weights.

Figure 11 provides a comparison of the simulated paediatric Ctrough,ss and Cmax,ss for paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt;12 years compared with the reference ranges for adult subjects based on the sensitivity analysis model using allometric exponents. The median Ctrough,ss was overall higher for all paediatric weight ranges. There was an increase in median Cmax,ss with decreasing body weight and the median Cmax,ss exceeded the 222 ng/mL limit for QT prolongation for the lowest body weights (12 kg to &lt; 15 kg).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The dashed purple line represents 222 ng/mL (the value at which the upper bound of the 2-sided 90% confidence interval for baseline-adjusted, placebo-corrected QT interval corrected using Fridericia's method is 10 msec [m2.7.2, Section 3.2.2.2]).

## 5.2.3. Pharmacodynamics

No new pharmacodynamic data was submitted as part of this application.

Paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years with HAE are expected to respond similarly to adults and adolescent patients aged 12 to &lt; 18 years treated with berotralstat if they achieve similar systemic exposure (PIP P/0151/2024). For this reason, it is important to maintain high enough Ctrough to maintain adequate kallikrein inhibition. Suboptimal levels of kallikrein inhibition associated with lower plasma concentrations may potentially impact efficacy. In Study 302, a placebo-controlled study in adult and adolescent patients aged 12 years and older, the 150 mg dose group had higher berotralstat plasma concentrations and greater levels of kallikrein inhibition than the 110 mg dose group as assessed by both the percentage of subjects with average concentrations at least 6-fold above the ex vivo EC50 and the percentage of subjects with &gt; 50% inhibition. The higher berotralstat concentrations and greater kallikrein inhibition in the 150 mg dose group resulted in a treatment effect of greater magnitude than in the 110 mg dose group. The MAH considers that the optimization of Ctrough is thus a critical factor to optimizing the efficacy of berotralstat in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years, and the use of Ctrough as the primary PK parameter to match adult exposures.

## 5.2.4. Pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics (PK/PD)

No new PK/PD analyses were submitted as part of this application.

## 5.2.5. Dose selection and therapeutic window

In PPK analysis, body weight was found to be a statistically significant modifier of berotralstat PK. Based on simulations using the final PPK model, steady-state exposures for paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years with HAE following daily dosing with 150 mg commercial capsule (i.e., without any dose adjustment for body weight) are expected to result in changes displayed in the Table 13 below. Dose adjustment based on body weight is therefore considered warranted for paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years.

## Monte-Carlo Simulations to justify berotralstat doses for paediatric participants with HAE (bcx7353-ms-002)

To support berotralstat dose ranges for paediatric participants with HAE, a series of Monte Carlo simulations were undertaken. The aim of these simulation analyses was to confirm doses that resulted in berotralstat exposures in paediatric patients similar to those in adults. In particular, the primary goal was to ensure that the 5th percentile of simulated Ctrough,ss for paediatric patients matched that of adults, such that, at most 5% of paediatric patients are expected to fall below the 5th percentile of target Ctrough,ss and that the median Ctrough,ss in paediatric patients is similar to adults. The doses and weight bands were also selected such that the secondary dose selection criterion that may be associated with safety, 95th percentile of the simulated Cmax,ss, were also similar to those of adults while also keeping the median Cmax,ss similar.

Monte-Carlo simulations using the final Population PK model were used to generate exposures for the doses and weight bands used in Study BCX7353-304, outlined in Table 14. The exposures following doses of 108 mg oral granules to 32 to &lt; 40 kg, 96 mg oral granules to 24 to &lt; 32 kg and 78 mg oral granules to 12 to &lt; 24 kg in paediatric patients 2 to &lt; 12 years of age resulted in exposures comparable to adults, exposures following 150 mg blend-incapsule for body weight ≥ 40 kg were considerably higher as compared to adults. For this reason, simulations for other dosing scenarios were generated.
