## 5.2.6. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.6.1. Discussion

## Bioanalytical methods

The bioanalytical methods assessed previously, are considered relevant for this application.

It can be accepted that the performance of the assay for Study BCX535-304 is in line with the bioanalytical guidelines.

## Pharmacokinetic data analysis

Standard non-compartmental methods have been described for the PK data analysis of Study 114. These methods are considered acceptable for the objectives of the study.

Standard non-compartmental methods have been described for the PK data analysis of Study 304. In addition, a standalone popPK approach (BCX7353-MS-002) has been used to estimate additional PK parameters in paediatric patients with HAE using the paediatric data collected from Study 304.

## Evaluation and qualification of models

## OLA-MS-001

The MAH sufficiently justified the full extrapolation approach for the dose selection for paediatric subjects aged 2 to 12 years. Given the disease progression described in the literature and the mechanism of action, with no ontogenic factors identified for the proposed age group, the full extrapolation approach was acceptable for the dose selection for a clinical study.

The pharmacokinetic model is overall acceptable for performing simulations to guide dosage selection for a Phase 3 trial in paediatric patients aged 2 to 12 years. The model parameters are well estimated with low SE and acceptable CV. The standard GOF plots show that the model predicts the observed data reasonably, with a slight over prediction for the population at higher concentrations.

## BCX7353-MS-002

The objectives of this popPK analysis are to characterise the PK of berotralstat and to support berotralstat dose recommendation in paediatric patients 2 to &lt;12 years of age with HAE.

## Methodology

The methods and software for parameter estimation for the model are considered acceptable. The study selection criteria described by the MAH are considered acceptable given the purpose of the model. The final dataset included 12394 PK samples from 786 study participants. Paediatric data from Study 304 contributed with 218 PK samples (1.7%). The handling of outlier or erroneous data was adequately described in the report.

The covariate-PK parameter relationships of interest are overall acceptable. Formulation effect on relative bioavailability (Frel), Ka, and Tlag is of interest, given that the capsule and oral granules formulations did not demonstrate bioequivalence. Dose effect on Frel is of particular interest given the dose-dependent bioavailability seen with berotralstat and the lower doses proposed for paediatric subjects.

The model development process described by the MAH is sufficient and indicates good modelling practice was followed. The approach to the structural and statistical model development is adequately described and considered acceptable. The covariate testing approach is adequately described and follows good modelling practice. The model evaluation and validation steps are adequately described.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Results

## Base popPK model

The steps taken to develop the base model into the final model are considered robust and sufficient detail regarding the stepwise testing and acceptance/rejection at each step has been provided in the report. A 3-compartment model as used in OLA-MS-001 was tested by sensitivity analysis and demonstrated minimal changes in OFV and failed minimisation. Given a preference for parsimony in model development, the 2-compartment model is considered acceptable.

## Final popPK model

The development of the final popPK model was described adequately. The structural model is well justified, and the covariate model has been justified through prior experience, clinical relevance, and the covariate testing methodology. Age was not included or tested as a covariate which is considered acceptable, as there is no expected ontogenic differences expected between the 2 to &lt;12 year paediatric and the &gt;12 years adolescent and adult population in the PK of berotralstat.

In the final model, the following covariates were retained: effect of body weight on all apparent clearance and apparent volume of distribution parameters, formulation on the rate of absorption, lag time and relative bioavailability and dose on the rate of absorption and relative bioavailability. Estimated allometric exponents were selected for the final model, however it needs to be noted that their estimation is likely driven by the adult data which constitute the major part of the dataset. For the purpose of paediatric extrapolation, it is generally advised to use fixed theoretical exponents. The MAH has adequately justified that using estimated exponents demonstrated better model fit with the observed data compared to the fixed allometric exponents in this case. The MAH has additionally performed a sensitivity analysis reducing the amount of adult data in the model. It can be accepted that models with reduced adult data did not provide substantial improvements to model fit, and the models failed to successfully converge. pcVPCs with the reduced dataset models did not show any significant improvements to model fit, and so a reduction of the dataset was not considered necessary.

The model parameters have generally been estimated with high precision, with low relative standard error (%RSE) overall, with the exception of the exponent of body weight on Vc/F which is moderately high (40.58%). Regarding bioavailability (Frel) and the rapid absorption compartment, it is noted that both formulation and dose were included as covariates, and the exponents were precisely estimated (RSE &lt;20%). The IIV and IOV are well estimated, however the shrinkage is moderately high (40-60%) for each parameter except CL/F, however this is still considered acceptable. The error in the model is precisely estimated.

The GOF plots presented are overall acceptable and do not indicate any model misspecification. For the paediatric patients (aged 2 to &lt;12 years) the model underpredicts at the higher concentrations for both individual and population predictions &gt;200 ng/mL. For population predictions the trend for underpredicting starts close to &gt;100 ng/mL, however the underprediction is smaller at this level.

The MAH has provided pcVPCs stratified by a number of factors. Figure 5 shows pcVPCs stratified by the paediatric and adolescent/adult data, demonstrating a reasonably good fit for both the paediatric data from Study 304 and the adolescent/adult data. The central tendency and variability for the 95 th percentile are overall well predicted by the model, but the variability for the 5 th  percentile is underpredicted after 12 hours. In Figure 6, pcVPCs stratified by weight show a reasonably good fit for the central tendency in all weight groups, however the variability (5th and 95th percentiles) is not well predicted, being over- and under-predicted respectively. The observed paediatric data is overall less variable than the model predicts, however this can be considered acceptable as the data is contained within the bounds of the predictions.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Clinical relevance of covariates

The only significant covariate identified was body weight, where weights &lt;40 kg led to a significant (outside 0.8 - 1.2-fold of reference) increase in the evaluated exposure parameters compared to the typical individual. This is agreed by CHMP. There was a mild decrease seen with a dose of 75 mg and mild increase seen with weights of 50 kg, but these are not considered significant. It is of note that paediatric population was not a significant covariate, however this is in line with discussion provided by the MAH that there is no expected impact of ontogeny on the exposure of berotralstat.

In the more in-depth analysis into the impact of body weight as a covariate, it was clearly demonstrated that the 150 mg capsule formulation when administered to paediatric patients of any weight would result in a significant increase (&gt;1.25-fold) in all exposure parameters at steady state (Ctrough, Cmax, AUC0-24, Cavg). It can be accepted that this simulation demonstrates a need for lower dosing in the paediatric population aged 2 to &lt;12 years weighing ≥40 kg, as proposed by the MAH.

Nevertheless, the apparent requirement for a dose adjustment in paediatric patients 2 to 12 years weighing ≥40 kg compared to the adult and adolescent population &gt;12 years of age is unexpected, given that body weight is the only significant covariate on exposure. The MAH clarified that the difference in dosing between adult/adolescent and paediatric subjects weighing ≥ 40 kg is due to the distribution of body weights in these age groups, where median weights for adults (76.9 kg) and adolescents (54.5 kg) weighing ≥ 40 kg are expected to be higher than that for paediatrics weighing ≥ 40 kg (44.8 kg). The MAH has provided a comparison of simulated exposures that demonstrate that the ratio of medians for the Ctrough,ss and Cmax,ss for paediatrics and adolescents weighing ≥40 kg are similar at the proposed doses.

## Bioavailability

## Study BCX7353-114

The MAH has developed a formulation appropriate for paediatric population, granules for oral administration. Each berotralstat granule contains 6 mg berotralstat which allows dosing flexibility. The active ingredient and excipients in the granules formulation are the same ingredients and in the same ratios as in capsule formulation. The granule cores are coated for taste-making purposes.

The MAH's approach to assess the relative bioavailability between the berotralstat granules and capsules dosage form in Study 114 is considered acceptable given that the MAH is not aiming to demonstrate bioequivalence between the two formulations as the stated aim is to target a specific exposure (Ctrough,ss) leading to a pharmacodynamic effect. The relative bioavailability of the granules formulation identified in this study is accounted for in the popPK model.

The overall study design and methodology for Study 114 Part 2 is considered acceptable for the objectives of the study. The sample size for Part 2 is small, however for PK purposes &gt;12 evaluable subjects is considered acceptable. The multiple dose study design is acceptable for the objectives, and the sampling time-points are considered appropriate to capture the Cmax/Tmax and AUCtau. The washout period is considered sufficiently long for the long half-life of berotralstat.

Following the multiple dose administration of 150 mg berotralstat, the mean serum berotralstat concentration-time profiles were similar between the test 150 mg mini-tablets (Regimen E) and reference 150 mg capsule (Regimen D) formulations at Day 1 and Day 7. There were no notable differences for the majority of PK parameters between the test and reference regimens. In general, the exposure parameters AUCtau, AUC0-inf, and Cmax were overall lower for the test formulation than the reference at both Day 1 and Day 7.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The analysis to evaluate the relative bioavailability between the test and reference formulations of berotralstat compared Cmax and AUCtau at Day 1 and Day 7. The point estimates (90% CIs) for the ratio of geometric means were 86.2 (79.2 - 93.8) for Cmax and 91.5 (83.8 - 100) for AUCtau at Day 1 and 91.7 (86.8 - 96.9) for Cmax and 94 (90.0 - 98.1) for AUCtau at Day 7. Overall, this demonstrates that the bioavailability of the test formulation is relatively lower than the reference, as unity was not crossed at Day 1 or Day 7. The standard bioequivalence criteria of 80 - 125% was not met for the Cmax at Day 1, indicating that the formulations are not bioequivalent overall.

The analysis to evaluate the relative bioavailability of 300 mg mini-tablets with the 150 mg capsule formulation demonstrated that when dose normalised, the relative bioavailability of the 300 mg minitablets is &gt;100%. This indicates that berotralstat exposures are greater than dose proportional. This finding is consistent with results from a previous popPK model, which demonstrated dose-dependent bioavailability where higher doses demonstrate high bioavailability. This finding does not impact the overall conclusions of this study.

Although the bioequivalence criteria were met at Day 7, the granule formulation is not considered to be bioequivalent with the capsule formulation due to the failure to meet the bioequivalence criteria at Day 1. Additionally, the study design is acceptable for a relative bioavailability study but not for a bioequivalence study in line with EMA guidelines (CPMP/EWP/QWP/1401/98 Rev. 1). This can be accepted as the relative bioavailability between the granule and capsule formulations is accounted for in the popPK model.

## Pharmacokinetics in the target population

## Study BCX7353-304

The study design for Study 304 is overall acceptable for the PK objectives. The dose rationale for the study was adequately justified in OLA-MS-001. The study enrolled participants into 4 cohorts according to their body weight. The age of the study population ranged from 3 to 11 years and weight from 14.9 to 69.7 kg at baseline. The sampling time-points for Cohort 1/2/3 are sufficient to perform analysis of PK parameters using non-compartmental methods and for inclusion in an updated popPK model. The sampling time-points for Cohort 4 at Week 2 were not sufficient for non-compartmental analysis methods but can be considered sufficient to include in the updated popPK analysis. All enrolled subjects completed Week 2 and data from all except one subject were included in the concentration summary. The one subject was excluded due to missing doses that could have impacted concentration results.

Following the multiple dose administration of berotralstat to paediatric patients with HAE, the mean plasma berotralstat concentrations were overall similar between Cohort 2 (108 mg granules, 32 to &lt;40 kg) and Cohort 3 (96 mg granules, 24 to &lt;32 kg) at each time-point for the Week 2 visit. The plasma concentrations for Cohort 1 (150 mg capsule, ≥40 kg) are notably higher at each time -point, and continues to show increasing concentrations up to 6 hours, indicative of a longer Tmax. Due to the reduced number of samples it is difficult to compare Cohort 4 (78 mg granules, 12 to &lt;24 kg) with the other cohorts, however this cohort showed higher pre-dose plasma concentrations than Cohort 2 and 3 but lower than in Cohort 1. Very high berotralstat concentrations (above 400 ng/mL) were observed in two subjects in Cohort 1 (075-601 and 178-601) and one subject in Cohort 2 (above 500 ng/mL). The MAH has performed a thorough evaluation of the observed high concentration values in Study 304. All of these values occurred in patients receiving 150 mg capsule, which is not the recommended dose in paediatric posology. One of the subjects administered two doses before the measurement was taken with less than 24h apart, which could be the likely explanation for the high concentrations in that subject. For the other two subjects, no such circumstances around dosing, intrinsic or extrinsic factors were found as a likely explanation. No adverse events were reported for any of the subjects with reported high berotralstat concentrations. It was noted that similarly high concentrations were observed in adult subjects with HAE. Given that the recommended doses for paediatric patients are lower and better match the adult exposures, no additional safety concerns are expected.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The summary of berotralstat PK parameters in Table 11 for Cohorts 1/2/3 demonstrate similar PK parameters for Cohort 2 and 3, and substantially higher PK parameters (Cmax, Clast, Tmax, AUC0-last, AUC0-6) for Cohort 1. PK parameters for Cohort 4 were not calculated due to the lower number of datapoints.

The non-compartmental analysis performed for this study suggests that dosing in Cohorts 2 and 3 resulted in similar PK exposure, and the dosing for Cohorts 1 and 4 resulted in higher exposures. It is noted that the observed Cmax and Ctrough are higher than the simulated Cmax and Ctrough (OLA-MS-001) for each group. The analysis for this study gives an indication that dosing in Cohort 1 may be too high as the Cmax crosses the 222 ng/mL limit for elongation of the QTc interval. The data collected in this study were further analysed and assessed in the popPK analysis (BCX7353-MS-002) and cross-study comparison (BCX7373-MS-003).

## Prediction of individual berotralstat exposures for participants in Study BCX7353-304

The popPK model developed for BCX7353-MS-02 was used to simulate individual berotralstat exposure for participants in Study BCX7353-304. The model was used to simulate rich berotralstat plasma concentrations at steady state for each participant in Study BCX7353-304 and to calculate the following parameters: AUC0-24,ss, Cmax,ss, time to Cmax,ss, Cave,ss, and Ctrough,ss, CL/F and Vss/F. The simulated exposure parameters overall were in line with the non-compartmental analysis performed as part of Study BCX7353-304. It is noted that the simulated CL/F was overall lower for each cohort than was predicted by the final popPK model, however this can be explained by the patient bodyweights in the study, as body weight is a significant covariate on CL/F.

## BCX7353-MS-003

The comparison of studies in BCX7353-MS-003 provides evidence to support the MAH's proposed posology in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years, based on similarity of exposure to adults. For the reference population in this analysis, healthy adult subjects were selected from studies 106 and 116 since these studies provided rich PK sampling with 150 mg dose administered to steady-state. Previously, (during initial MAA) it was concluded that no significant differences in exposure were observed between healthy volunteers and HAE patients, therefore selection of the reference population can be agreed. However, concerns have been identified for some cohorts.

Plasma exposure parameters were slightly higher but similar overall in paediatric patients from Study BCX7353-304 compared to the adult exposure for Cohorts 2 and 3, with the ratio of geometric means falling between 1.01 and 1.19 for all parameters in these cohorts. For Cohort 4, only the Ctrough was calculated due to too few data points being taken to calculate other parameters and it was also similar to adults, but marginally lower. Plasma exposure parameters for Cohort 1, the cohort that received the 150 mg capsule, the same dose as adults and adolescents, demonstrated higher exposure parameters; the ratio of geometric means was 1.73 and 1.25 for the Cmax and Ctrough respectively.

The results of this analysis support that the recommended dosing in paediatric patients for Cohorts 2, 3, and 4 used in Study BCX7353-304 would be expected to achieve similar drug exposures in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 compared to adults. The 150 mg dose (in capsule) administered in Cohort 1 resulted in exposures that were consistently higher than exposures in the adult reference population; therefore, selection of the 150 mg dose for that body weight band in paediatric patients 2 to &lt; 12 years of age is not supported.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Modelling and simulation analyses (BCX7353-MS-002) were subsequently performed to support exposure matching and these analyses are considered pivotal for establishing dose regimen in the paediatric population aged 2 to &lt; 12 years.

## Exposure relevant for safety evaluation

It is noted from Table 14 that the 95th percentile for the Cmax,ss for each proposed dosing group is higher than the 222 ng/mL exposure limit for a significant QT prolongation (290 ng/mL for 132 mg granules in ≥40 kg, 274 ng/mL for 108 mg granules in 32 - 40 kg, 289 ng/mL for 96 mg granules in 24 - 32 kg, and 281 ng/mL for 72 mg granules in 15 to &lt;24 kg). The MAH has provided discussion that where the risk of QT prolongation is the safety parameter of interest, that the most relevant PK parameter to inform this is the Cmax,ss. The MAH has made this comparison with respect to the central tendency and outlined that the popPK model-predicted median Cmax,ss values are ≤ 165 ng/mL for all proposed dosage levels, which is below the 222 ng/mL threshold.

## Dose justification

## BCX7353-MS-002

To match exposure between adult and paediatric subjects, the following criteria were used:

- at most 5% of paediatric subjects could have Ctrough,ss below the 5th percentile of predicted Ctrough,ss in adults (parameter relevant for efficacy)
- median Cmax,ss of paediatric subjects is similar to median simulated Cmax,ss in adults (parameter relevant for safety)

The criteria for matching of Ctrough,ss parameter are very clear and supported, the criteria for matching Cmax,ss parameter are considered somewhat vague.

Table 14 details the simulated comparison of exposure parameters for paediatric patients 2 to &lt;12 year of age with HAE vs adults with the same dosing as Study BCX7353-304.  Each paediatric dosing scenario in Table 14 demonstrated ≤5% fell below the 5 th  percentile, indicating that each dose met this target. Additionally, each dosing scenario demonstrated a similar median Ctrough,ss to the 150 mg capsule in adults, except for the 150 mg capsule in paediatrics patients ≥40 kg which was notably higher. From this it could be accepted that only the dosing for the paediatrics ≥40 kg needed to be adjusted to a lower dose, compared to the doses investigated in Study BCX7353-304.

The MAH simulated a large number of potential dosing scenarios Table 15 summarises the dosing scenarios of interest for the MAH proposed posology, and additionally dosing scenarios of interest for the assessment. For the dosing scenarios of interest for the MAH's initially proposed posology, it could be accepted that the simulations for the scenarios (132 mg QD oral granules for patients with body weight ≥ 40 kg, 108 mg QD oral granules for patients with body weight 32 to &lt; 40 kg, 96 mg QD oral granules for patients with body weight 24 kg to &lt; 32 kg, and 78 mg QD oral granules for patients with body weight &lt; 24 kg) appear to somewhat meet the target exposures stated by the MAH. With the initially proposed posology, simulated median Cmax,ss in paediatric subjects was 1.17- to 1.36-fold higher than the median Cmax,ss in adult subjects and 8.9 to 19.5% of paediatric patients are expected to have Cmax,ss above the 95th percentile of Cmax,ss in adult patients. The proposed doses result in high exposures, making extrapolation of safety uncertain, especially in the youngest paediatric patients with body weights &lt; 24 kg.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In line with this, the MAH has proposed an updated dose of 72 mg for a new weight band with a lower limit of 15 to &lt; 24 kg. The simulated Cmax,ss for the newly proposed group showed a 1.17-fold higher median Cmax,ss, with 9.2% of this group demonstrating a Cmax,ss higher than the 95 th  percentile of the adult group adult group. The simulated parameters for this group were better aligned with both the simulated adult and the other proposed dosing groups.

Although setting the lower body weight limit (15 kg) is understood given the studied body weight range (&gt; 14.9 kg), according to the CDC growth charts, the typical body weight range for children 2 years of age would be approximately 10 - 15 kg, with average around 12 kg. Therefore, setting the lower body weight boundary to 15 kg would likely exclude the majority of youngest patients. The MAH provided simulations with the same updated posology (72 mg QD) for the 12 to &lt; 24 kg group. According to the simulations provided (Table 14), for the body weight band 12 to &lt; 24 kg, 6.1% of paediatric patients would have Ctrough,ss below the 5th percentile of predicted Ctrough,ss in adults, compared to 6.8% for the earlier proposed body weight band of 15 to &lt; 24 kg. For the body weight band 12 to &lt; 24 kg, median Cmax,ss (164 ng/mL) was somewhat higher than for the body weight band of 15 to &lt; 24 kg (155 ng/mL). This extrapolation to subjects weighing 12 to &lt;15 kg was supported by a sensitivity analysis performed with model run 6100 using fixed exponents for weight on Vd and CL.

The MAH additionally provided Figure 10 and Figure 11, which presents the simulations for all paediatric weight ranges and their respective doses as on a continuous scale, with summary statistics (median, 5 th  and 95 th  percentile) binned by 2 kg ranges, allowing a closer inspection of exposure parameters than provided by the 12 kg to &lt; 24 kg range, for the final model and sensitivity analysis respectively. Based on the simulations from the final model, the Ctrough,ss for all weight and dose groups are in line with the reference range seen in adults. The simulations also demonstrate that although there is a trend of increasing median Cmax,ss with decreasing body weight, the median Cmax,ss does not exceed the 222 ng/mL limit for even the lowest body weight bin (12 to 14 kg).

However, for the extrapolation outside of studied body weight range, the use of fixed exponents would be preferred as a more conservative approach. Even though the simulations using the model with estimated exponents confirm that median Cmax,ss values for 12 to &lt;15 kg group would remain below the threshold concentration, of 222 ng/mL in the sensitivity analysis using the model with fixed exponents the median Cmax,ss values for 12 to &lt; 15 kg cross this threshold. Therefore, as a measure of precaution, extrapolation for the 72 mg dose to the 12 to &lt;15 kg group is not supported. The MAH has implemented the lower body weight to 15 kg in the indication.

To enable a dose recommendation to an even greater proportion of paediatric subjects aged 2 years and older, the MAH was requested to simulate exposures for a new weight band for subjects weighing &lt; 15 kg at a lower dose (e.g. 66 mg QD for subjects weighing 9 to &lt; 15 kg). The MAH has provided discussion that extrapolation to paediatric body weights below 12 kg is not supported by the popPK model using fixed exponents for body weight (run 6100), as the geometric mean for Cmax,ss were similar to or above the 222 ng/mL threshold for QT prolongation. Additionally, in terms of clinical impact, the MAH's argument that the youngest and lightest patients (e.g., those who recently turned 2 years) typically utilize on-demand treatment and do not require long-term prophylaxis for HAE attacks, can be accepted. However, given that the mean weight of infants aged 2 years is approximately 12 kg it is considered important that the MAH further explores dosing in this subgroup. Therefore, the CHMP concluded that a recommendation [REC] for the MAH  to further investigate lower dosing regimens in paediatric subjects weighing 12 to &lt; 15 kg by performing additional simulations with lower dosing regimens for these weights would be adequate.

It is noted that additional dosing bands for paediatric patients &gt; 40 kg were investigated with simulations. It can be accepted that these dosing bands (≥ 50 kg and 40 kg to 50 kg) did not provide any substantial improvements to the exposure parameters compared to the ≥40 kg dosing band.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Therefore, it could be agreed that based on these simulations using the final popPK model that additional dosing bands for paediatric patients ≥ 40 kg are not required to achieve the Ctrough,ss target.

The MAH has also proposed in the SmPC Section 4.2 for Orladeyo granules that it is recommended that in patients 12 years and older with a body weight less than 40 kg, a dose of one 108 mg sachet of berotralstat granules once daily may be considered. Given the relationship between bodyweight and exposure is not impacted by age this could be accepted. The MAH also provided simulations to support this approach in adolescents weighing &lt; 40 kg receiving 108 mg berotralstat. The simulated exposures in this group were comparable to exposures in adults. The recommendation that berotralstat 108 mg granules can be considered in adolescents aged greater than 12 years with a body weight &lt; 40 kg is considered justified.

## Intrinsic and extrinsic factors

No dedicated DDI, RI or HI studies were performed in the paediatric population, and this is acceptable.

Since Cmax,ss in paediatric subjects using the proposed posology is expected to be somewhat higher than in adult patients, and an additional increase is expected in patients with severe renal impairment with unknown impact on safety, the use of berotralstat should be avoided in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years with severe renal impairment. This is adequately addressed in the SmPC.

## Pharmacodynamics

No PD data was collected in paediatric population in Study 304, despite being advised so in the PIP.

No PD data on kallikrein inhibition in the paediatric population is seen as a limitation of the programme, since there was no confirmation of the hypothesis that response to treatment is similar between paediatric and adult patients. However, given the rarity of the disease in the younger children, common pathogenesis of the disease between paediatric and adult patients and since descriptive efficacy data support reduction of the HAE attack rate, extrapolation approach based on exposure matching can be accepted.

## 5.2.6.2. Conclusions

The clinical pharmacology data, including the population PK model and simulations, support the dosing regimen based on weight bands in children aged 2 to less than 12 years weighing at least 15kg for the granules for the routine prevention of recurrent attacks of hereditary angioedema.  The agreed posology for the children and adolescent population is as follows:

- For a body weight of 15 kg to less than 24 kg, the recommended dose of Orladeyo granules is one 72 mg sachet once daily

- For a body weight of 24 kg to less than 32 kg, the recommended dose of Orladeyo granules is one 96 mg sachet once daily

- For a body weight of 32 kg to less than 40 kg, the recommended dose of Orladeyo granules is one 108 mg sachet once daily

- For a body weight of 40 kg or greater, the recommended dose of Orladeyo granules is one 132 mg sachet once daily

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.3. Clinical efficacy

## 5.3.1. Dose response study

For dose rationale, refer to section 5.2.

## 5.3.2. Main study

## 5.3.2.1. Study BCX7353-304

## 5.3.2.1.1. Study title

Study BCX7353-304:  A Phase 3 study to evaluate the safety and pharmacokinetics of berotralstat prophylaxis in children with hereditary angioedema who are 2 to &lt; 12 years of age. (APeX-P). (Ongoing).

Table 16. Study identifiers

| Study code          | BCX7353-304    |
|---------------------|----------------|
| EU CT number        | 2021-005932-50 |
| NCT number          | NCT05453968    |
| Other identifier(s) | APeX-P         |

## 5.3.2.1.2. Study design

This is an ongoing, sequential, 3-part, open-label study to evaluate the PK, safety, and efficacy/effectiveness of berotralstat in participants with HAE who were 2 to &lt; 12 years of age at enrolment. Participation in this study was expected to be a minimum of 12 weeks in the standard of care (SOC) period, 12 weeks on berotralstat in Part 1 of the study, and up to an additional 132 weeks in Parts 2 and 3. The SOC period served as a control for safety assessments and provided additional data regarding baseline attack rates in this population.

Figure 12. Study schema

<!-- image -->

data

Abbreviations: BSLN = baseline; DC = discontinue; Enr = enrollment; P/C = parents and/or caregivers; PK = pharmacokinetic; QD = once daily; SOC = standard of care; Wk = week.

a) Subjects were enrolled at the screening visit. If, after the screening visit, laboratory results were returned that indicated the subject no longer met the inclusion and exclusion criteria, the subject was considered a screen failure.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- b) Indicates visits where single, random, plasma sample was collected for PK.

An interim Clinical Study Report (CSR) with data cutoff date: 11 September 2024 and a CSR Addendum with a data cutoff date of 17 September 2025, has been submitted for Study 304, in support of this application. The Study 304 Interim CSR detailed the pharmacokinetic (PK), safety, efficacy/effectiveness, and palatability results of Study 304 after at least 15 participants completed 48 weeks of treatment (through Part 2) to support regulatory filings (interim data cutoff date: 11 September 2024).

Efficacy results are presented through an interim data cutoff date of 24 March 2025 after all participants remaining on study had completed the efficacy observation period (Week 48, end of Part 2). No additional efficacy data were collected after this date.

Total duration of berotralstat treatment may be up to 144 weeks: 12 weeks in Part 1; 36 weeks in Part 2; and up to an additional 96 weeks in Part 3.

## 5.3.2.1.2.1. Treatment

The study enrolled participants into 4 cohorts of decreasing dose of berotralstat. Participants were assigned into each cohort based on participant weight on Day 1. The participant doses and weight bands for each cohort were determined based on PK modelling and simulation as presented in the Table 17 below.

## 5.3.2.1.2.2. Randomisation

Study BCX7353-304 is an open label study. Randomization and blinding are not applicable.

## 5.3.2.1.2.3. Patient population

Male and female participants aged 2 to &lt; 12 years of age weighing ≥ 12 kg with a clinical diagnosis of HAE who met the specified biochemical testing requirements with regard to C1 esterase inhibitor (C1INH) antigenic level, C1-INH function and/or complement component 4. Diagnosis of HAE in participants may also have been confirmed by historical or new laboratory documentation of a SERPING-1 mutation known or likely to be associated with HAE or, for participants receiving C1-INH prophylaxis, a confirmed family history of C1-INH deficiency.

## 5.3.2.1.3. Objectives and estimands

## 5.3.2.1.3.1. Primary objective

To describe the PK parameters of berotralstat administered orally to paediatric participants with HAE aged 2 to &lt; 12 years old and weighing ≥ 12 kg.

## Primary Endpoint

The primary endpoint was the characterization of the PK profile of berotralstat in participants aged 2 to &lt; 12 years.

## 5.3.2.1.3.2. Estimands for the primary objective

No formal hypotheses are being tested in this study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

No imputation was done for missing data. No adjustment for multiple comparisons was done since inferential testing was not planned for this study. No subgroup analyses were planned. No stratification or adjustment for covariates was planned. No formal hypotheses were tested in Study BCX7353-304.

## 5.3.2.1.3.3. Secondary objectives

## Secondary Objectives

- To assess the safety and tolerability of berotralstat administered orally to paediatric participants with HAE aged 2 to &lt; 12 years old and weighing ≥ 12 kg
- To summarize the effectiveness of berotralstat in paediatric participants with HAE aged 2 to &lt; 12 years old and weighing ≥ 12 kg

## Secondary Endpoints

- The secondary endpoint of safety was measured by frequency and severity of adverse events (AEs) and serious adverse events (SAEs), laboratory analyses (clinical chemistry, haematology, coagulation), height, weight, vital signs, electrocardiograms (ECGs), and findings from physical examinations (PEs).
- Safety endpoints are assessed at the end of each part (ie, Weeks 12, 48, and 144/end of study [EOS]).
- The other secondary endpoint of effectiveness is measured by assessing the frequency (number and rate) of HAE attacks, duration of symptoms, anatomical location, number and proportion of attacks requiring on-demand treatment, number and proportion of days with angioedema symptoms, assessment of attack severity, discontinuations due to lack of efficacy, and number of hospitalizations and clinic visits from Week 1 through Weeks 12 and 48.

## Exploratory Objective

- To assess the acceptability/palatability of berotralstat oral granules in paediatric participants with HAE aged 2 to &lt; 12 years old and weighing ≥ 12 kg

## Exploratory Endpoints

Acceptability/palatability of the berotralstat oral granules was assessed in participants receiving the oral granules using an age-appropriate scale by site personnel at the time of first dose (Day 1) for Cohorts 2, 3, and 4.

## 5.3.2.1.3.4. Estimands for the secondary objective

No formal hypotheses are being tested in this study.

## Primary Efficacy Variable

Evaluation of the effectiveness of berotralstat was a secondary objective in this study. The effectiveness analysis was conducted on the safety population and comprised multiple endpoints as listed below:

- Frequency (number) and rate of HAE attacks
- Attack duration
- Anatomical location of attacks

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Number and proportion of attacks requiring on-demand treatment
- Number and proportion of days with angioedema symptoms
- Assessment of attack severity
- Discontinuations due to lack of efficacy
- Number of hospitalizations and clinic visits (per month)

Additional details were provided in the Statistical Analysis Plan (SAP).

## Statistical methods for estimation and sensitivity analysis on the secondary estimands

No imputation was done for missing data. No adjustment for multiple comparisons was done since inferential testing was not planned for this study. No subgroup analyses were planned. No stratification or adjustment for covariates was planned. No formal hypotheses were tested in Study BCX7353-304.

## 5.3.2.1.4. Results

For primary PK and secondary safety outcomes of Study BCX7353-304, refer to sections 5.2 and 5.4.

Effectiveness was observed up to 48 weeks (Parts 1 and 2 of the study). Of the 29 enrolled participants, 17 participants reached the Week 48 visit prior to the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date. All participants remaining on study have now completed a Week 48 visit; therefore, all effectiveness data collection in Study 304 is now complete.

## 5.3.2.1.4.1. Participant flow and numbers analysed

A total of 32 participants were screened, of which, 29 participants were enrolled at 11 sites located in 9 countries: 19 participants were enrolled in the EU (Austria, France, Germany, Italy, Poland, and Spain), 5 participants in the UK, 3 participants in Canada, and 2 participants in Israel.

Of the 32 screened participants, 3 participants screen failed; 2 participants withdrew consent prior to the Day 1 visit, and 1 participant did not meet weight requirements for the available open cohort. None of the participants screen failed due to an inability to meet the inclusion/exclusion criteria.

All 29 enrolled participants received berotralstat, including 7 participants in Cohort 1, 9 participants each in Cohorts 2 and 3, and 4 participants in Cohort 4, based on their weight at Day 1.

At the time of the interim data cutoff date of 17 September 2025, all 29 enrolled participants (100%) had completed Part 1 of the study (through Week 12), and Part 2 of the study (through Week 48), with 27 participants (93.1%) in the week 48 Completer Set. Across all cohorts, 5 participants had discontinued the study, including 3 participants (10.3%) due to perceived lack of efficacy, 1 participant (3.4%) due to withdrawal by participant on Day 195 following lack of compliance with prescribed dosing and 1 participant due to 'other'. One participant discontinued the study during Part 3 due to parent's decision based on availability of an alternative prophylaxis therapy. As per the CSR addendum, two additional participants discontinued berotralstat during Part 3 but had not yet completed the EOS visit.

Of the 5 participants who discontinued the study, 2 discontinued before Week 48 and 3 participants discontinued after Week 48. All participants remaining in the study (24 participants [82.8%]) were in Part 3 (Weeks 48 through 144). No participants had yet completed the study. The patients not completing Part 2 of the study (through Week 48) were both in Cohort 2 (n=2/9), with discontinuation prior to Week 48.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Nineteen participants have experienced at least 1 dose modification or interruption during the study. As per the interim CSR, nine participants had dose modifications while on study due to weight increases (n = 7), principal investigator (PI) medical management of an AE (n = 1), and sponsor decision based on serum berotralstat concentrations (n = 2) as allowed by the Study Protocol. One of the 9 participants that had dose modifications experienced 1 dose modification for a weight increase and 1 dose modification for PI management of an AE. Data for these participants are presented under the same cohort in which they were enrolled based on their original assigned dose regardless of dose adjustments made during the study. Therefore, while cohorts do not reflect a universal dosing regimen among all included participants, exposure levels to berotralstat are expected to be similar among all participants as a result of these dose modifications.

As per the CSR addendum, dose modifications were reported for a total of 16 participants since the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date. Dose modifications were due to weight increases (n = 10), medical management of an AE (n = 2), and other (n = 4), i.e. Sponsor decision based on PK data, switch from capsule to granules due to difficulty swallowing, protocol amendment and unknown.

Of note, the dose modifications for 2 of the 16 affected participants occurred prior to the interim data cut on 11 September 2024.

## Protocol Deviations

After sponsor review of the major deviations, 6 important protocol deviations were identified (5 participant-level in 4 participants and 1 study-level deviation), including missed doses that affected drug exposure and noncompliance with required dose adjustments. Additionally, two participants had informed consent signed by only one parent instead of both, as required by local regulations. At the study level, a significant deviation occurred as activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT), a required coagulation test, was not measured by the central laboratory for any samples collected before May 8, 2024. This affected all participants and visits from the study's start in October 2022, including baseline assessments.

## Treatment Compliance

Across all cohorts, the median overall compliance rate was 97.30% (range: 73.5 to 100.1%), with 24 participants (82.8%) reporting compliance of 90% or higher.. As of the interim data cutoff date for the CSR Addendum (17 September 2025), overall treatment compliance for all but 2 participants (6.9%) was 80% or higher. Median compliance from Weeks 13 through 48 (Part 2) was similarly high (97.20% [range: 82.7 to 100.4%]), with 24 participants (82.8%) reporting compliance of 90% or higher. No participants reported Part 2 compliance &lt; 80%. There were no discernible differences in compliance with berotralstat dosing between cohorts. One participant in total (Cohort 2) discontinued from the study due to withdrawal by participant following lack of compliance as reported in the Study 304 Interim CSR. No additional participants withdrew due to lack of compliance.

Those with confirmed compliance &lt; 90% or &gt; 100% between 2 visits were reported as protocol deviations. No additional important deviations related to dosing compliance were reported in the CSR addendum since the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.3.2.1.4.2. Deviations from study plan

The original Protocol dated 15 December 2021 (Version 1.0) underwent 5 global amendments (Versions 2.0, 3.0, 4.0, 5.0, and 6.0) and 4 country-specific amendments for Canada, Germany, the UK, and Italy (Versions 1.1, 1.2, 1.3, and 1.4, respectively).

The key changes in Protocol Version 7.0 dated 05 November 2024, applying to the CSR Addendum, are clarifying guidance for discontinuing study intervention when specific indicators of drug-induced liver injury are observed to align the protocol with EU Clinical Trials regulation 536/2014.

## 5.3.2.1.4.3. Baseline data

Overall, 29 participants were included in the ITT, safety, and PK populations. All 29 participants were included in the Week 12 completer population (100%) completed Part 1 of the study, and 27 of 29 participants (93.1%) were included in the Week 48 completer set. No participants were screened or enrolled following the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date.

## Disease-related Baseline Characteristics

## HAE Diagnosis and Symptom Onset

In accordance with Inclusion Criterion 3, a clinical diagnosis of HAE could have been confirmed in a variety of ways. A summary of the confirmation of clinical diagnosis of HAE for the participants in the safety population is presented in the Table 19 below. In Study 304, all enrolled participants were qualified as having a clinical diagnosis of HAE on the basis of low C1-INH function level at screening or on the basis of historic laboratory documentation, and all participants met at least 1 diagnostic criteria for Type 1 or 2 HAE as required by the Study Protocol. All participants reported C1-INH function &lt; 50% at screening or through historical laboratory documentation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Genetic mutation information was available for 3 of the 4 participants with historical SERPING-1 genetic results. Three of the 4 participants for whom SERPING-1 genetic mutation information was available provided genetic sequence information. The mutations documented for 2 participants are published mutations known or likely to be associated with HAE (p.Gly184Arg and Pro467Arg) (p.Gly184Arg-ClinVar Miner; Pedrosa et al. 2016). The mutation documented for 1 participant appears to be a novel mutation in SERPING-1 (C.889+2T&gt;G). Mutation information for 1 participant was not consented.

## Historical HAE Attack Rates

All subjects reported at least 2 HAE attacks in the 6 months prior to enrolment. Subjects reported a median of 11 attacks in the 6 months prior to enrolment (range: 2 to 24 attacks) or 1.8 attacks/month (range: 0.3 to 4 attacks/month).

## Location and Symptoms of Pre-study HAE Attacks

In the prior year, the most common anatomic region where swelling occurred was the stomach/abdomen (89.7%), and correspondingly the most common symptoms of an HAE attack were abdominal pain (79.3%), vomiting (69.0%), and nausea (62.1%). Visible swelling was also observed in the hands and arms (72.4%), feet and legs (65.5%), and face/head (55.2%) during an HAE attack in the past year.

Pre-study laryngeal attacks were reported less frequently but still occurred in 4 subjects (13.8%). The mean (standard deviation) estimated number of laryngeal attacks subjects had ever experienced was 1.5 (0.58) attacks; of the 4 subjects who had laryngeal attacks in the past, 3 reported having a laryngeal attack in the past year.

Pre-Study HAE Treatment and Medical Visits for HAE

Pre-study healthcare utilization in this study population was high, indicating a high disease burden in the enrolled patient population. Approximately half of the subjects (15 of 29 subjects [51.7%]) required a median of 3.0 emergency room visits (range: 1 to 43 emergency room visits), and 5 of 29 subjects (17.2%) reported a median of 3.0 hospitalizations (range: 1 to 8 hospitalizations), due to HAE symptoms in the prior year.

## Concomitant Medications for HAE and Acute Treatment of Angioedema Attacks

Concomitant medications other than those specified in the HAE attack diary that were taken by &gt; 20% of participants in all cohorts were ibuprofen and paracetamol across the entire berotralstat treatment period.

Two participants (1 participant each from Cohort 2 and Cohort 3) used tranexamic acid as an ondemand HAE treatment while on study, as reported in the Study 304 Interim CSR. One additional participant in Cohort 4 has reported use of C1 esterase inhibitor ([C1-INH], Berinert) as short-term prophylaxis (for a bump on the forehead) since the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date for a total of 3 participants who used C1-INH for short-term prophylaxis while on study. One participant in Cohort 3 started using lanadelumab following discontinuation of berotralstat but prior to the EOS visit.

There are no notable differences between cohorts in the use of concomitant medications.

The Table 21 below summarizes concomitant medications used by participants for acute treatment of angioedema attacks during the berotralstat treatment period. In Parts 1 and 2 of the study, 24 participants (82.8%) reported at least 1 adjusted attack, with a total of 162 adjusted attacks reported across all participants. Although multiple participants had Cinryze or Ruconest available, only Berinert and Firazyr were used for on-demand treatment. Non-targeted HAE medications, including tranexamic acid, were used in 3.1% of adjusted attacks reported on study.

## 5.3.2.1.4.4. Outcomes and estimation

## Efficacy outcomes Study-304 (secondary endpoints)

Use of prophylaxis during the SOC period

The use of prophylaxis during the SOC period was allowed per protocol and may have impacted the overall baseline attack rate. Five subjects used long-term prophylaxis (LTP) HAE medication during the SOC period, including 4 subjects who used tranexamic acid daily and 1 subject who used lanadelumab. Three subjects had access to Berinert for short-term prophylaxis as needed, 2 of whom used tranexamic acid for LTP. All LTP treatments were stopped prior to starting berotralstat on study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The 12-week SOC period was followed by the berotralstat treatment period (Parts 1, 2, and 3).

## Number of adjusted HAE attacks

A total of 135 adjusted attacks were reported during the 12-week SOC period across all subjects (Table 22, Table 23, Table 24 below). Only 50 adjusted attacks were reported in the first 12 weeks on berotralstat treatment (Part 1) (Table 22, Table 23, Table 24 below), with 162 reported through Parts 1 and 2 as of the interim data cutoff date of 17 September 2025 (Table 22, Table 23, Table 24 below).

A higher proportion of subjects were attack free during Parts 1 and 2 of the study than in the 12-week SOC period:

- 1 subject (3.4%) reported no adjusted attacks during the 12-week SOC period
- 12 subjects (41.4%) reported no adjusted attacks from Day 1 through Week 12 (Part 1)
- 5 subjects (17.2%) reported no adjusted attacks from Day 1 up to Week 48 (Parts 1 and 2)

## Rate of adjusted HAE attacks

Relative reduction in adjusted attack rate for Part 1 and during Parts 1 and 2, respectively were presented. Relative reduction is defined as (baseline attack rate - adjusted Week 12 attack rate) × 100/baseline attack rate.

Summary of HAE adjusted attack rate in the safety population:

- During the 12-week SOC period, the median attack rate (based on all 29 evaluable subjects) was 0.96 attacks/month (range: 0.00 to 5.03 attacks/month).
- During Part 1, the median attack rate was 0.33 attacks/month (range: 0.00 to 2.33 attacks/month). The median attack rate was 0.339 (0.00, 3.82) attacks/month during Part 2.
- Overall (Parts 1 and 2), the median attack rate was 0.417 attacks/month (range: 0 to 1.77 attacks/month).

The median attack rate was lower in Part 1, Part 2, and during Parts 1 and 2 of the study than observed at baseline (SOC period). Subjects reported a median 63.5% reduction in attack rate through Week 12 relative to baseline and 68.9% reduction in attack rate up to Week 48 relative to baseline.

One subject in Cohort 2 reported substantially higher attack rates during Parts 1 and 2 than baseline. This subject used lanadelumab LTP during the SOC period and reported only 1 adjusted attack in this period. This subject later discontinued due to perceived lack of efficacy between Weeks 13 and 48.

Relative reduction from baseline in mean adjusted HAE attack rates for Week 12 completer population was 19% for Cohort 1, 58% for cohort 2, 36% for Cohort 3 and 70% for Cohort 4 approx.

Relative reduction from baseline in median adjusted HAE attack rates for Week 12 completer population was 16% for Cohort 1, 100% for Cohort 2, 90% for Cohort 3 and 63% for Cohort 4.

All cohorts experienced meaningful reductions from baseline. Cohort 1 was the only cohort in which the mean adjusted attack rate exceeded the baseline level at any assessed timepoint- at Month 3. Specifically, for Cohort 1, the baseline mean attack rate was 1.188 (SD 0.8325), increasing to 1.571 (SD 2.0702) at Month 3. However consistent reductions from baseline were reported thereafter in Cohort 1 from Month 4 through to Week 48, 0.385 (SD 0.6881).  Cohort 2 demonstrated an initial reduction to a median of 0.0 at Week 12, with an increase to 0.423 by the end of the study, indicating early improvement followed by greater variability, one participant in Cohort 2 reported substantially higher attack rates during Parts 1 and 2 than baseline, for further details see Discussion.

## Adjusted HAE attack rates over time

Low median attack rates were observed from 1 month after starting treatment with berotralstat and remained low each month during Parts 1 and 2 (up to Week 48).

Median attack rates across the safety population decreased from 0.96 attacks/month (range: 0.00 to 5.03 attacks/month) at baseline (SOC period) to 0.00 attacks/month starting at Month 1 on treatment and remained at 0.00 attacks/month for each month through Parts 1 and 2 (up to Week 48) with the exception of Month 6 (median 0.500 attacks/month).

The mean (± standard error of the mean [SEM]) and median total adjusted HAE rate for all 29 subjects in the safety population are plotted by month in Figure 14 and Figure 15 below (A and B, respectively). Mean (± SEM) attack rates across the safety population decreased from 1.51 (± 0.24) attacks/month at baseline (SOC period) to 0.52 (± 0.17) attacks/month at Month 1 on treatment and remained lower than baseline through Parts 1 and 2 (up to Week 48).

Monthly attack rates within each cohort were variable. No cohorts reported an increased median change from baseline (SOC period) attack rates in any month during Parts 1 and 2 (through Week 48). No cohorts reported an increased mean change from baseline (SOC period) attack rates in any month during Parts 1 and 2 (through Week 48) except in Cohort 1 at Month 3.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Duration of adjusted attacks

The median duration of adjusted attacks was similar at baseline (SOC period) (18.0 hours [range: 0.5 to 129.0 hours]), through Week 12 (17.2 hours [range: 0.5 to 80.0 hours]), and up to Week 48 (18.63hours [range: 0.3 to 132.0 hours]).

Consistent with the Study 304 Interim CSR, median (range) attack duration for Cohort 4 (5.00 hours [1.5, 42.0] hours) was shorter than in Cohort 1 (19.00 [0.3, 99.5] hours), Cohort 2 (20.50 [1.5, 132.0] hours) and Cohort 3 (20.00 [1.3, 72.5] hours), although this observation could be confounded by the small cohort size (N = 4).

Through Week 48, median (range) duration of abdominal-only attacks (14.00 [0.5, 132] hours) was shorter than peripheral-only (32.50 [2.8, 99.5] hours) and laryngeal-only attacks (37.49 [4.0, 99.5] hours).

Abdominal-only attacks occurring in Cohort 4 participants up to Week 48 were generally shorter (median [range] of 3.00 [2.5, 14.0] hours) than other cohorts (median [range] of 16.51 [0.5, 57.0] 19.00 [1.5, 132.0], and 14.50 [1.3, 42.5] hours for Cohort 1, 2, and 3, respectively. This observation is reflected in the median attack duration among all attacks in Cohort 4 relative to other cohorts. Laryngeal attacks were infrequently reported, precluding any meaningful comparison between cohorts.

## Attack locations for adjusted attacks

Berotralstat treatment did not discernibly alter the location of HAE attack symptoms experienced by subjects. The reduction in attack number and rate was generally similar regardless of attack location.

Laryngeal attacks included events with visible swelling in the mouth/tongue/lips or any of the following symptoms: lump in throat (tightness), change in voice, difficulty swallowing, or difficulty breathing. Note that although swelling of the lips is not generally considered a laryngeal symptom, subject diaries did not discriminate between swelling of the lips from that of the mouth or tongue and were considered symptomatic of a laryngeal attack, consistent with the definition used in prior berotralstat studies.

## On-demand HAE medication

All subjects had access to approved acute medications for the treatment of HAE attacks during the study.

The overall median rate of attacks requiring targeted treatment decreased from 0.69 attacks/month (range: 0.00 to 5.03 attacks/month) at baseline (SOC period) to 0.30 attacks/month (range: 0.00 to 2.33 attacks/month) through Week 12 and remained low through Week 48 (median of 0.169 attacks/month [range: 0.00 to 1.75 attacks/month]) (Table 28 below). Targeted HAE medications used to treat attacks occurring in this study included Berinert and Firazyr, see Section 6.3.2.1.4.3 for further details.

Cohort 1 reported the highest rate of attacks requiring targeted treatment through Week 48, with median (range) 0.417 (0.00, 1.00) attacks/month. Each cohort exhibited low attack rates for attacks requiring targeted treatment during Part 2 of the study, although this measure was lower for Cohort 3 than other cohorts, with median (range) of 0.00 (0.00, 1.58) attacks/month. A summary of HAE adjusted attack rates for attacks requiring treatment with targeted HAE medications by study part is presented in the Table 28 below.

## Number and proportion of days with angioedema symptoms

During the 12-week SOC period, the median percentage of days with angioedema symptoms was 9% (range: 0% to 40%) and was generally similar across cohorts. The median percentage of days with symptoms decreased to 2% (range: 0% to 20%) during Part 1 and 2% (range: 0% to 20%) during Part 2. Overall (Parts 1 and 2), the median percentage of days with symptoms was 3% (range: 0% to 20%). The percentage of days with symptoms was similarly low from Day 1 through Week 48, with a median (range) of 3% (0, 10).

During the 12-week SOC period, the median number of days with angioedema symptoms was 8.0 (range: 0, 35). The median number of days with symptoms decreased to 2.0 (range: 0, 15) during Part 1 and 6.0 (range: 0, 27) during Part 2. Overall (Parts 1 and 2), the median number of days with symptoms was comparable to baseline 9.0 (range: 0, 42). Cohorts 3 and 4 reported fewer median days with symptoms (5.0 and 6.5, respectively) than Cohorts 1 and 2 (13.0 and 11.0, respectively). This is consistent with trends observed at baseline (SOC period).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Similar proportions of attacks were rated by the subject as negligible or mild in severity at baseline (SOC period) and during Parts 1 and 2 of the study. Overall, there was a lower rate of severe attacks while on treatment relative to SOC.
