## Discontinuation due to lack of efficacy

No subjects discontinued treatment due to perceived lack of efficacy through Part 1 (Week 12). One subject (3.4%) in Cohort 2 discontinued treatment due to perceived lack of efficacy in Part 2, between Weeks 13 and 48.

## Number of hospitalizations and professional care visits

During the 12-week SOC period, professional medical care (defined as at either a physician's office, urgent care, emergency room, or home) was sought for a total of 22 attacks (16.3%) across the safety population. The majority of these attacks were experienced by subjects in Cohort 3 (19 of 22 attacks). No subjects were admitted to the hospital as a result of an adjusted attack.

Three attacks (6.0%) required professional medical care during Part 1 of the study. Nine attacks (5.6%) required professional medical care during Parts 1 and 2. The majority of these attacks were experienced by subjects in Cohort 3 (5 of 9 attacks). Subjects across all cohorts reported fewer attacks and a lower proportion of attacks that required professional medical care during Parts 1 and 2 of the study than during the SOC period. No subjects were admitted to the hospital as a result of an adjusted attack in the SOC period or during Parts 1 and 2 of the study.

The median rate of adjusted attacks requiring hospitalization or medical care was 0 attacks/month among the subjects in the safety population and within each cohort during the SOC period as well as through Weeks 12 and 48 of treatment.

## 5.3.3. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical efficacy

## 5.3.3.1. Discussion

This application is based on paediatric extrapolation using pharmacokinetic (PK) and safety data to match effective and safe exposures in the adult (reference) population as substantial evidence in supporting the safe and effective use of berotralstat granules for oral administration in the paediatric population aged 2 to &lt; 12 years with HAE, in accordance with the agreed Paediatric Investigation Plan (P/0151/2024), with additional simulation/comparison supporting the use of the granules in patients older than 12 years weighing less than 40 kg submitted during the procedure. Study BCX7353-304 (Study 304), a Phase 3 open-label study was designed to evaluate the PK, safety, and effectiveness of berotralstat for prophylaxis in paediatric subjects aged 2 to &lt; 12 years of age with HAE. Given the differences in study designs and subject populations, information from Study 304 has not been integrated with prior berotralstat clinical studies in adults and adolescents.

## Study BCX7353-304

Study design comprised of 12 weeks in the standard of care (SOC) period, which served as a control for safety assessments, and provided additional data regarding baseline attack rates in this population, followed by 12 weeks on berotralstat in Part 1 of the study, and up to an additional 132 weeks in Parts 2 and 3. The study enrolled participants into 4 cohorts of decreasing dose of berotralstat. Participants were assigned into each cohort based on participant weight on Day 1. The participant doses and weight bands for each cohort were determined based on PK modelling and simulation. During the SOC period participants were allowed to continue the use of any prior ongoing prophylactic medication to help minimize the rate of HAE attacks, however harmonization of background therapy across the population was not required, thus hampering the comparison between participants. During Part 1 and onward, subjects received only berotralstat for long-term prophylaxis (LTP).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The primary objective of this study was to describe the PK parameters of berotralstat administered orally in paediatric participants with HAE aged 2 to &lt; 12 years old and weighing ≥ 12 kg. Secondary objectives were to assess the safety, tolerability, and effectiveness of berotralstat for LTP of HAE attacks in this population. Outcomes for PK and safety are assessed in Sections 5.2 and 5.4 respectively.

Efficacy endpoints were evaluated through Week 48. These included the frequency, number, and rate of HAE attacks; duration of symptoms; anatomical location of attacks; and the number and proportion of attacks requiring on-demand treatment. Additionally, researchers assessed the number and proportion of days with angioedema symptoms, attack severity, discontinuations due to lack of efficacy, and recorded the number of hospitalizations and clinic visits.

Acceptability/palatability was assessed as an exploratory endpoint using an age-appropriate hedonic scale for the oral granule formulation by those participants who received the oral granule formulation. No patient-reported outcomes (e.g. Angioedema Quality of Life Scale implemented during MAA) were included in this clinical program. Given the significant burden that HAE poses on patients and their families, the absence of such data is considered a limitation of the clinical program for this age group.

Eligible participants were male or female subjects aged 2 to less than 12 years, weighing at least 12 kg, with a clinical diagnosis of HAE. Inclusion required meeting specific biochemical criteria for C1 esterase inhibitor (C1-INH) levels, function, and/or complement component 4, or confirmation of a relevant SERPING-1 mutation. For those on C1-INH prophylaxis, a documented family history of C1INH deficiency was also accepted. Participants not on prophylaxis must have had documented history of ≥ 2 HAE atta cks in the 6 months prior to the enrolment visit. Overall, the eligibility criteria are considered acceptable regarding the target population.

In the pivotal studies for marketing authorisation of Orladeyo in adults and adolescents (Study BCX7353-302 and 301), participants were required to have had a documented attack rate of at least 2 attacks per month within 6 months prior to screening, or ≥ 2 attacks during a 56-day run-in period. In Study 304, as per eligibility criteria, participants did not need to have a minimum number of attacks in the SOC period prior to receiving berotralstat. This would have enabled a participant to enter Part 1 and 2 of the study even if they were attack free in the SOC period, which potentially hampers the direct comparison of efficacy between periods.

## Results

At the time of the interim data cutoff date of 17 September 2025 (CSR addendum), all 29 enrolled participants (100%) had completed Part 1 of the study (through Week 12), and 27 of 29 participants (93.1%) completed Part 2 of the study (through Week 48). 5 participants had discontinued the study, 2 discontinued before Week 48 and 3 participants discontinued after Week 48. All participants remaining in the study (24 participants [82.8%]) were in Part 3 (Weeks 48 through 144). No participants had yet completed the study (Part 3).

## Baseline Demographics

Participants in the safety population were enrolled at 11 sites in 9 countries, including sites in Canada, Israel, the UK, and the EU. Twenty-nine participants were enrolled across 4 cohorts and assigned an initial dose (78 mg, 96 mg, 108 mg, or 150 mg) of berotralstat QD based on weight at baseline. Seven participants were enrolled in Cohort 1, 9 participants in Cohort 2, 9 participants in Cohort 3, and 4 participants in Cohort 4.

With the exception of race and ethnicity, the safety population was generally diverse with an age range of 3 to 11 years old and weights ranging from 14.90 to 69.70 kg; no participants under 3 years of age were enrolled.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

By design, Cohort 1 participants had the highest weight at baseline, decreasing through Cohort 4. Age, BMI, and height followed a similar trend as expected based on the associated weight bands required for enrolment into these cohorts. Because Cohort 1 did not have an upper weight limit, baseline weight in Cohort 1 had a greater range (40.19 kg to 69.70 kg) than the other cohorts, which had Protocolspecified weight ranges (Cohort 2 [32 to &lt; 40 kg], Cohort 3 [24 to &lt; 32 kg], and Cohort 4 [12 to &lt; 24 kg]. The applicant's argument that the pathophysiology of HAE of a 2-year-old is similar to that of a 3year-old is accepted.

Enrolment was approximately even between males and females (51.7% and 48.3%, respectively). Notably, all individuals in Cohort 4 were male. All participants who reported known race and ethnicity were White and not Hispanic or Latino. The distribution of age, weight, and sex are reflective of the targeted population and previous studies conducted on adult and adolescent participants with HAE and therefore appropriate for assessment of berotralstat in paediatric patients with HAE.

## Disease History and Baseline Severity

Most participants (89.7%) reported a family history of Type 1 or 2 HAE as expected given the autosomal dominant inheritance pattern of HAE.

The majority of participants in this study experienced an onset of first symptoms &lt; 6 years of age (37.9% of participants from birth to &lt; 2 years of age and 44.8% of participants from 2 to &lt; 6 years of age), substantially earlier than puberty. Median age for onset of HAE symptoms in participants was 2 years.

All subjects reported at least 2 HAE attacks in the 6 months prior to enrolment. Subjects reported a median of 11 attacks in the 6 months prior to enrolment (range: 2 to 24 attacks) or 1.8 attacks/month (range: 0.3 to 4 attacks/month) and 4 participants (13.8%) reporting laryngeal attacks at any time prior to enrolment.

In the past year, Cohorts 1 to 3 showed very high swelling rates in the stomach/abdomen (86% to 100%), whereas Cohort 4 had slightly less abdominal swelling (75%) but uniquely high swelling in hands and arms (100%) and notable involvement of buttocks/genitals (75%). Swelling of the face/head was more prominent in Cohorts 2 and 3, less so in Cohort 4. Feet and legs swelling was common but varied moderately across groups. Regarding other attack symptoms, abdominal pain was universally common, peaking at 100% in Cohort 3. Nausea and vomiting were especially frequent in Cohorts 2 and 3, each reaching nearly 90%, while fatigue was more pronounced in Cohorts 1, 3, and 4. Diarrhoea appeared less often but affected about half the participants in Cohorts 2 to 4. Notably, erythema marginatum was reported only in Cohort 3 (56%), highlighting a distinct symptom prevalence there.

In terms of healthcare utilization; 51.7% participants required a median of 3.0 ER visits per year and 5 participants (17.2%) required a median of 3.0 hospitalizations, due to HAE symptoms in the prior year.

Only 2 subjects (6.9%) did not miss any days of education in the year prior to enrolment. The remaining 27 subjects (93.1%) missed at least 1 day of education. The median number of missed days due to HAE symptoms was 10.0 (range: 0 to 90 missed days).

No LTP was approved for patients &lt; 6 years of age at the time of study start in the studied regions. Despite the high disease burden, a minority of participants (37.9%) reported using prophylaxis at any time, primarily Berinert, which is approved down to 6 years of age in the studied regions, and tranexamic acid, which is not recommended for HAE prophylaxis, and none had used androgens. Even fewer (5 of the 29 participants) had access to LTP during the SOC period.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The majority of participants (22 participants [75.9%]) treated at least 1 HAE attack with a targeted on-demand HAE medication or a medication to manage attack symptoms during the berotralstat treatment period. Only Berinert and Firazyr were used for on-demand treatment.

Overall, the demographics and clinical characteristics were appropriate for assessing berotralstat in paediatric HAE patients.  Per request, the Applicant provided a tabulated summary of comorbidities reported as medical history for subjects in Study 304. The most frequently reported comorbidities across cohorts were allergies, particularly seasonal and plant allergies. Few participants reported more serious conditions such as type 1 diabetes mellitus, epilepsy, asthma and hemiparesis (which was ongoing during the study). Overall, there were no significant differences between cohorts, and most comorbidities were typical for paediatric population.

## Discontinuations

5 participants discontinued the study, including 3 participants (10.3%) due to perceived lack of efficacy, 1 stated as due to other and 1 participant (3.4%) due to withdrawal by participant on Day 195 following lack of compliance with prescribed dosing. One participant discontinued the study during Part 3 due to parent's decision based on availability of an alternative prophylaxis therapy. Of the 5 participants who discontinued, 2 discontinued before Week 48 and 3 participants discontinued after Week 48. All participants remaining in the study (24 participants [82.8%]) were in Part 3 (Weeks 48 through 144).

Notably, one participant in Cohort 2 reported substantially higher attack rates during Parts 1 and 2 than baseline and discontinued treatment in Part 2 due to perceived lack of efficacy. No demographic or baseline disease characteristics were identified that differentiated this subject from the overall cohort. A detailed subject narrative was provided. Treatment compliance was 100%, and pharmacokinetic (PK) results were within normal limits. The only factor distinguishing this subject from the remainder of the cohort was prior treatment with lanadelumab, to which the subject had responded well during the 12-week standard of care period. Given that the subject's disease was well controlled with lanadelumab relative to the overall study population, it could be accepted that the perception of efficacy may not have aligned with that of other subjects in Study 304.

## Study -304 Efficacy Outcomes

Efficacy data were presented for Week 12 completer population which included all 29 participants (100%) and Week 48 completer population which included 27 of 29 participants (93.1%).

Results of the interim data analysis showed that subjects treated with berotralstat 150 mg capsules (Cohort 1) or granules (Cohorts 2, 3, and 4) demonstrated low HAE attack rates through Week 12 (Part 1) that persisted throughout Part 2 of the study during which effectiveness was monitored (up to Week 48).

Comparison of efficacy between the SOC period and during Parts 1 and 2 of the study is limited due to the following:

- The SOC period did not require harmonization of background therapy across the population. Subjects were allowed to continue on their chosen HAE medications without restrictions, which results in a nonuniform comparison between subjects. Use of LTP or short-term prophylaxis in the SOC period may have reduced the attack rate of subjects, thus decreasing the potential apparent reduction in disease burden experienced during the treatment period.
- Eligibility was based on pre-study historic attack rate or ongoing LTP for an individual subject; therefore, subjects did not need to have a minimum number of attacks in the SOC period prior to receiving berotralstat. This would have enabled a subject to enter Parts 1 and 2 of the study

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

even if they were attack free in the SOC period and limited the ability to make direct effectiveness comparisons.

During the SOC period, 29 participants experienced a total of 135 adjusted HAE attacks across 4 cohorts: Cohort 1 (26 attacks), Cohort 2 (46 attacks), Cohort 3 (49 attacks), and Cohort 4 (14 attacks), with one participant in Cohort 1 reporting no attacks. In Part 1 (Day 1 to Week 12), 50 attacks were recorded, distributed as: Cohort 1 (20), Cohort 2 (8), Cohort 3 (17), and Cohort 4 (5), with 2 participants in Cohort 1, 5 in Cohort 2, 4 in Cohort 3, and 1 in Cohort 4 experiencing no attacks. Over Parts 1 and 2 (Day 1 to Week 48), 162 attacks were reported: 43 in Cohort 1, 51 in Cohort 2, 49 in Cohort 3, and 19 in Cohort 4.

Although the effectiveness analysis is descriptive in nature, collected effectiveness data suggested a trend of lower disease burden for subjects while on treatment with berotralstat relative to SOC, as demonstrated through:

- lower rates of attacks (median 0.96 attacks/month during the SOC period, 0.33 attacks/month during Part 1, and 0.417 attacks/month during Parts 1 and 2),
- Relative reduction from baseline in mean adjusted HAE attack rates for Week 12 completer population was 19% for Cohort 1, 58% for cohort 2, 36% for Cohort 3 and 70% for Cohort 4.
- Mean relative reduction from baseline for Week 48 completer population was 67% for Cohort 1, 38% for Cohort 2, 62% for Cohort 3 and 50% for Cohort 4.
- a lower proportion of days with HAE symptoms (9% during the SOC period, 2% during Part 1, and 3% during Parts 1 and 2).
- berotralstat treatment produced a very modest reduction in rate of attacks requiring targeted treatment (135 adjusted attacks were reported during the SOC period, of which 115 (85.2%) were treated with targeted acute HAE medication. By Week 12, 41 of 50 attacks (82.0%) in the completer population received targeted medication, with similar rates across all cohorts. During the Day 1-Week 48 treatment period, 127 of 162 attacks (78.4%) were treated with targeted medication.)
- lower rate of severe attacks (32.6% during the SOC period, 16.0% during Part 1, and 29.0% during Parts 1 and 2), and
- less professional healthcare was sought to manage HAE symptoms (22 attacks [16.3%] during the SOC period, 3 attacks [6.0%] during Part 1, and 9 attacks [5.6%] during Parts 1 and 2). No subjects were admitted to the hospital as a result of an adjusted attack in the SOC period or during Parts 1 and 2 of the study.

## Number and rate of adjusted HAE attacks, attack rates over time per cohort

A total of 135 adjusted attacks were reported during the 12-week SOC period, 50 in the first 12 weeks (Part 1) and 162 reported through Parts 1 and 2 as of the interim data cutoff date of 17 September 2025. The number of subjects with at least 1 adjusted attack was 28 (69.6%) at baseline (SOC period), 17 (58.6%) in Part 1 (12 weeks) and 24 (82.8%) through Parts 1 and 2 (up to Week 48).

Monthly attack rates within each cohort were variable; however, all cohorts experienced meaningful reductions from baseline, see above. Similar trends were seen when reporting by median attack rates. Cohort 1 was the only cohort in which the mean adjusted attack rate exceeded the baseline level at any assessed timepoint. Specifically, for Cohort 1, the baseline mean attack rate was 1.188 (SD 0.8325), increasing to 1.571 (SD 2.0702) at Month 3, however consistent reductions from baseline were reported thereafter in Cohort 1 from Month 4 through to Week 48, 0.385 (SD 0.6881).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Trends observed during Parts 1 and 2 of the study are generally consistent across all cohorts and doses tested, indicating potential similar levels of effectiveness across the age and weight range included in this study. Discontinuations impacting efficacy outcomes were minimal. Of the 5 participants who discontinued, 2 discontinued before Week 48 and 3 participants discontinued after Week 48.

## Duration, locations and severity of adjusted attacks

The median duration of adjusted attacks was similar at baseline (SOC period) (18.0 hours [range: 0.5 to 129.0 hours]), through Week 12 (17.2 hours [range: 0.5 to 80.0 hours]), and up to Week 48 (19.0 hours [range: 0.3 to 132.0 hours]).

Median (range) attack duration for Cohort 4 was shorter than in Cohorts 1, 2, and 3, but this observation could be confounded by the small cohort size (N = 4) and younger age.

Through Week 48, median (range) duration of abdominal-only attacks (14.00 [0.5, 132] hours) was shorter than peripheral-only (32.50 [2.8, 99.5] hours) and laryngeal-only attacks (37.49 [4.0, 99.5] hours).

Berotralstat treatment did not discernibly alter the location of HAE attack symptoms experienced by subjects. The reduction in attack number and rate was generally similar regardless of attack location.

During the SOC period, 53.6% of participants reported experiencing abdominal-only attacks, compared to 64.7% during Part 1 and 75.0% during Parts 1 and 2. Non-abdominal peripheral attacks were reported by 60.7% of participants during the SOC period, compared with 35.3% during Part 1 and 62.5% during Parts 1 and 2. Mixed location attacks were reported by 53.6% of participants during the SOC period, versus 52.9% during Part 1 and 54.2% during Parts 1 and 2. With respect to laryngeal attacks, an increasing trend in the proportion of participants reporting these attacks was observed as the study progressed, with 14.3% during the SOC period, 17.6% during Part 1, and 29.2% during Parts 1 and 2. Of note, swelling of the mouth/tongue/lips while on study was considered symptomatic of a laryngeal event. This was a conservative estimate that likely overestimated the percentage of laryngeal attacks reported on study and may not be consistent with the participant-reported history of laryngeal attacks collected at screening. As requested, the Applicant explained that the apparent rise in laryngeal attacks over time is due to methodological factors. The reported figures reflect the proportion of participants with at least one adjusted attack who experienced laryngeal symptoms, not the total study population. When recalculated by total population, proportions are 13.8% (SOC), 10.3% (Part 1), and 24.1% (Parts 1+2), showing a different trend. The increase through Week 48 reflects longer follow-up and is not clinically meaningful. Attack-level analysis offers a truer assessment, as subject-level data cannot separate single from recurrent events. Across all study parts, laryngeal symptoms occurred in 5.2% (SOC), 6.0% (Part 1), and 4.9 % (Parts 1+2) of attacks, confirming consistent efficacy of berotralstat in reducing attack frequency, including potentially lifethreatening laryngeal attacks.

Analysis of attack severity across the study reveals that moderate attacks consistently represented the largest proportion in each period. During the SOC period, the proportion of severe attacks was notably higher, accounting for 32.6% of attacks. In contrast, during Part 1 of the study, the proportion of severe attacks decreased significantly to 16.0%, representing a relative reduction of 50.9% compared to the SOC baseline. When considering both Parts 1 and 2 (a total of 48 weeks), severe attacks accounted for 29.0% of the total, reflecting a relative reduction of 11.0% compared to the SOC period. The substantial reduction in severe attacks observed during Part 1 was not fully sustained throughout the entire 48-week period. This trend suggests an overall shift toward less severe attack profiles during treatment, particularly evident during Part 1, although the effect appeared to diminish somewhat over time.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Exploratory endpoint: Acceptability/Palatability of Berotralstat Oral Granules

The acceptability and palatability of berotralstat oral granules were evaluated in Cohorts 2, 3, and 4 on Day 1 after dosing using the TASTY scale, which ranges from 0 to 6, with 6 indicating the most favorable taste. Overall, a moderate level of acceptability and palatability was observed, with a median TASTY score of 3.0 (range 0 to 6).

Overall, results were comparable to reductions seen in the adult population (Study 302). To note, Berotralstat treatment did not discernibly alter the duration of attacks and location of HAE attack symptoms experienced by subjects. In addition, while a substantial reduction in severe attacks was observed during Part 1, this was not fully sustained throughout the entire 48-week period.

Although the effectiveness analysis is descriptive in nature, collected effectiveness data suggested a trend of lower disease burden for subjects while on treatment with berotralstat relative to SOC. Berotralstat demonstrated a durable and clinically meaningful reduction in HAE attack frequency across cohorts, despite significant variability in individual responses across all time points, as shown by high standard deviations and wide interquartile ranges.

## 5.3.3.2. Conclusions on the clinical efficacy

Given of the rarity of the disease, the study design and the relatively low number of subjects included are considered acceptable.

Overall, collected efficacy data in Study BCX7353-304 suggested a clinically meaningful trend of lower disease burden for subjects while on treatment with berotralstat compared  to SOC. These findings support the efficacy of the berotralstat in reducing attack frequency in the paediatric population.

## 5.4. Clinical safety

For the purpose of this document, the following definitions apply:

'Adverse event - AE' means any untoward medical occurrence in a subject to whom a medicinal product is administered and which does not necessarily have a causal relationship with this treatment.

'Serious adverse event - SAE' means any untoward medical occurrence that at any dose requires inpatient hospitalisation or prolongation of existing hospitalisation, results in persistent or significant disability or incapacity, results in a congenital anomaly or birth defect, is life-threatening, or results in death. The definition (in line with ICH E2A) includes important medical events that may not be immediately life-threatening or result in death or hospitalisation but may jeopardise the patient or may require intervention to prevent one of the other outcomes listed in the definition above.

'Adverse Drug Reaction - ADR' means any untoward and unintended response to a medicinal product related to any dose administered, for which, after a thorough assessment, a causal relationship between the medicinal product and the adverse event is at least a reasonable possibility, based for example, on their comparative incidence in clinical trials, or findings from epidemiological studies and/or on an evaluation of causality from individual case reports.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.1. Study BCX7353-304, Phase 3 (ongoing)

## 5.4.1.1. Safety data collection

The secondary endpoint of safety was measured by frequency and severity of adverse events (AEs) and serious adverse events (SAEs), laboratory analyses (clinical chemistry, hematology, coagulation), height, weight, vital signs, electrocardiograms (ECGs), and findings from physical examinations.

Safety endpoints are assessed at the end of each Part (i.e. Weeks 12 [Part 1], 48 [Part 2], and 144/end of study).

The other secondary endpoint of effectiveness is measured by assessing the frequency (number and rate) of HAE attacks, duration of symptoms, anatomical location, number and proportion of attacks requiring on-demand treatment, number and proportion of days with angioedema symptoms, assessment of attack severity, discontinuations due to lack of efficacy, and number of hospitalizations and clinic visits from Week 1 through Weeks 12 and 48.

## 5.4.1.3. Adverse events

The system organ classes with the highest number of participants reporting TEAEs were Infections and infestations (20 participants [69.0%]), Gastrointestinal disorders (8 participants [27.6%]) and Respiratory, thoracic and mediastinal disorders (8 participants [27.6%]). The PTs of nasopharyngitis (10 participants [34.5%]) and upper respiratory tract infection (8 participants [27.6%]) were the most frequently reported TEAEs.

Up until the 11 Sept 2024 interim data cutoff date, six participants (20.7%) reported TEAEs in the GI disorders system organ class, including 2 of 9 participants (22.2%) in Cohort 2 and 4 of 9 participants (44.4%) in Cohort 3. No participants in Cohorts 1 or 4 reported TEAEs in this system organ class. Vomiting was the most frequently reported event in the system organ class and was experienced by 3 participants (10.3%), including 1 participant in Cohort 2 and 2 participants in Cohort 3. Two participants (6.9%), both in Cohort 3, reported TEAEs of abdominal pain (3 events) and 2 participants (6.9%), also in Cohort 3, reported TEAEs of nausea. No other TEAEs in this system organ class were reported by more than 1 participant.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

After the 11 Sept 2024 interim data cutoff date vomiting remained the most frequently reported event in this system organ class and was experienced by 5 participants (17.2%) overall, 2 of whom (1 participant each in Cohorts 2 and 4) reported this TEAE since the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date. The TEAEs of vo miting were transient with relatively short duration (≤ 4 days), self - limiting, and did not result in disruption of study intervention. One participant (Cohort 1) reported a TEAE of abdominal pain and 1 participant (Cohort 4) reported a TEAE of diarrhoea since the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date. No additional participants reported TEAEs of nausea. No other TEAEs in this system organ class were reported by more than 1 participant.

No increase in the severity of berotralstat GI TEAEs was noted in paediatric participants compared with those seen in adult and paediatric participants 12 years of age and older.

## Comparison of Adverse Events during the Standard of Care (SOC) Period and TEAEs during Part 1 (Safety Population)

The SOC period served as a control for safety assessments during Part 1 of the berotralstat treatment period. AEs by system organ class that were experienced by participants during the 12-week SOC period and Part 1 of the study were similar. The most frequent events experienced by the participants by system organ class in both periods are typical of AEs generally seen in the paediatric population such as Infections and infestations; Respiratory, thoracic and mediastinal disorders; and GI disorders.

## Adverse Events by Severity

The majority of TEAEs were Grade 1 or Grade 2 in severity. A single participant (Cohort 1) experienced Grade 3 TEAEs of accident and upper limb fracture by PT that met the criteria for an SAE. No additional Grade 3 or 4 TEAEs were reported up until the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date.

Of the 6 participants (20.7%) who experienced TEAEs in the GI disorders system organ class, 4 participants (13.8%) had Grade 1 TEAEs and 2 participants (6.9%) had Grade 2 TEAEs by highest severity. Additional information on the Grade 2 TEAEs is provided below:

· One participant in Cohort 3 experienced TEAEs of Grade 2 vomiting and nausea, both of which started on Day 36. The events were not related to treatment. No changes to berotralstat dosing occurred due the events, and both the events resolved on the same day.

· One participant in Cohort 2 experienced a TEAE of Grade 2 food poisoning starting on Day 10. The event was not related to treatment. No changes to berotralstat dosing occurred due to the event, which resolved on Day 12.

All TEAEs reported after the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date were Grade 1 or 2 in severity and no new Grade 3 or 4 TEAEs were reported.

## Treatment-emergent Adverse Events by 100 Person-Years of Exposure (PYE)

TEAEs were also evaluated by calculating the rate per 100 PYE to account for differential observation time bias between individual participants. PYE is calculated for each participant who completed or discontinued the study as (last dose date - first dose date + 1) / 365.25 and for each ongoing participant as (11 September 2024 - first dose date + 1) / 365.25 and then summed across all participants in a given dose group. All events (multiple occurrences of the same event in a participant) are represented in the rate of events per 100 PYE.

Overall, the results of the 100 PYE analyses were consistent with what was observed for TEAEs and TESAEs reported in the Study 304 Interim CSR. No new safety signals were observed

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.1.3.1. Adverse drug reactions

Overall, 2 participants (6.9%) experienced 2 TEAEs related to study intervention (up until the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date):

· One participant in Cohort 3 experienced an event of Grade 1 nausea starting on Day 4. The berotralstat dose was not changed, and the event of nausea resolved on Day 43.

· One participant in Cohort 2 experienced an event of Grade 2 headache starting on Day 28. The berotralstat dose was not changed, and the event of headache resolved on Day 41.

No Grade 3 or Grade 4 TEAEs related to study intervention were reported.

Overall, 2 participants reported 2 TEAEs related to study intervention after the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date:

· One participant in Cohort 2 experienced an event of Grade 1 vomiting. The berotralstat dose was not changed, and the event resolved without intervention. Note: this TEAE was in response to taking crushed granules, in which the taste-masking coating of the granules was compromised. Therefore, this TEAE likely represents an acute reaction to the bitter taste of the oral granules rather than the GI AE associated with berotralstat.

· One participant in Cohort 3 experienced an event of Grade 1 weight increased starting on Day 336. The berotralstat dose was not changed, and the event resolved on Day 546.

No Grade 3 or 4 TEAEs related to study intervention were reported.AEs of special interest, serious adverse events and deaths, other significant events

For this Protocol, no events of special interest were identified. AEs in the Hypersensitivity, Anaphylactic Reaction, Severe Cutaneous Adverse Reaction Standardized Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities [MedDRA] Queries (SMQs) and Torsade de Pointes (TdP)/QT prolongation SMQ or a treatmentemergent adverse event (TEAE) with the preferred terms (PTs) of palpitation or seizure were not designated as AEs of special interest in the Protocol but were monitored closely for safety.

To date, a total of 9 TEAEs were reported under the Hypersensitivity Standardized MedDRA Query (SMQ), 9 TEAEs in the Anaphylactic reaction SMQ, and 0 TEAEs in the Severe cutaneous adverse reactions SMQ during berotralstat treatment. Overall, 9 participants experienced 15 TEAEs under these 3 SMQs. Four TEAEs were reported after the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date and included rhinitis allergic (3 events in 2 participants) and rash (1 event). All TEAEs were assessed as not related to the study intervention by the investigator.

There were no TEAEs from the TDP/QT prolongation SMQ or palpitation PT. Also, no seizure events were reported.

No deaths were reported.

## Other Serious Adverse Events

Only 1 participant in Cohort 1 experienced 2 SAEs of Grade 3 accident and upper limb fracture during Part 2 of this study up until the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date.

Following the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date, one Cohort 1 participant experienced a TESAE of Grade 2 hepatitis acute, and one Cohort 3 participant experienced a TESAE of Grade 1 hemiparesis. Both SAEs were assessed as not related to study intervention.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.1.3.2. ADRs of special interest, serious ADRs and deaths causally related to the medicinal product.

Four SAEs were reported in 3 participants and were assessed as not related to study intervention. Berotralstat did not result in severe or serious adverse reactions in participants enrolled in the study.

## 5.4.1.4. Discontinuation due to adverse events

Overall, no TEAEs led to discontinuation of the study intervention.

One participant experienced a TEAE that led to a dose modification. One participant was initially assigned to Cohort 2 based on weight. This participant received berotralstat 108 mg oral granules QD starting Day 1. On Day 169, the dose was increased to 150 mg capsule QD due to weight change. On Day 175, a TEAE of Grade 2 affect lability was reported. The PI suspected the TEAE of affect lability was an emotional reaction to the formulation change from the oral granules (which the participant had become familiar with during the study) to the 150 mg QD capsules, based on communication with the participant's family. Therefore, the participant was returned to the berotralstat dose of 108 mg oral granules QD on Day 225 to continue on the granules. The participant's status was stable, and no increase of attacks was observed after the dose reduction to the 108 mg oral granules. The reason for the reduction was listed as PI medical management. This TEAE was deemed not related to berotralstat by the PI. The event of affect lability resolved on Day 232 (after 58 days).

Treatment interruption (33 days) was reported for 1 participant who experienced acute hepatitis (PT: hepatitis acute).

## 5.4.1.5. Vital signs and laboratory findings

## Clinical Chemistry

## Laboratory Values Over Time

No notable changes from baseline were observed.

## Individual Participant Changes

For treatment-emergent toxicity shifts in clinical chemistry parameters from baseline (defined as the latest non-missing result prior to administration of the first dose of berotralstat) to worst post-baseline assessment, except as otherwise described up until the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date, most abnormal laboratory parameters were a 1- or 2-grade shift from baseline.

No participant had an elevated creatinine or a low CLCR value at any time during the study. However, some participants had CLCR that met a Division of Acquired Immunodeficiency Syndrome (DAIDS) graded abnormality. DAIDS CLCR toxicity grading is based on percent change and not applicable for the paediatric participants in this study who had low baseline serum creatinine. Serum creatinine in the paediatric population is generally low. The baseline range in study participants was 0.2 to 0.6 mg/dL. Minimum detectable changes in serum creatinine (0.1 mg/dL) in this population resulted in a large change in CLCR that was not clinically meaningful and did not represent changes in renal function. Grade 3 and 4 changes in CLCR observed in this study were transient in nature and were deemed not clinically significant by the investigators. Of note:

- Cohort 2: 1 of 9 participants (11.1%) experienced a grade shift in CLCR from normal at baseline to Grade 3.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Creatinine values for this participant changed from 0.2 mg/dL at Day 1 to 0.3 mg/dL at all timepoints tested.

- Cohort 3: 1 of 9 participants (11.1%) experienced a grade shift in CLCR from normal at baseline to Grade 4.

- Creatinine values for this participant changed from 0.3 mg/dL at Day 1 to 0.6 mg/dL at Week 48.

- Cohort 4: 2 of 4 participants (50.0%) in Cohort 4 experienced a grade shift in CLCR from normal at baseline to Grade 3.

- Creatinine values for Participant changed from 0.2 mg/dL at Day 1 to 0.3 mg/dL at Weeks 6 and 24.

- Creatinine values for Participant changed from 0.2 mg/dL at Day 1 to 0.3 mg/dL at Weeks 6, 12, and 24.

One of 9 participants (11.1%) in Cohort 1 had a transient shift in cholesterol from normal at baseline to Grade 2 at Week 48 (202 mg/ml), which returned to normal in 8 weeks. Cholesterol results could have been impacted by DAIDs version 2.1 grading using the fasted state. Fasting prior to labs was not required by the Study Protocol.

Except as otherwise described, most abnormal laboratory parameters were a 1- or 2-grade shift from baseline. Only worst post-baseline shifts of 3 grades or higher reported since the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date are discussed herein.

- One of 29 participants had a shift in alanine aminotransferase (ALT), aspartate aminotransferase (AST), and bilirubin from normal at baseline to Grades 4, 3, and 3, respectively, at Day 573. These shifts are associated with the Grade 2 TESAE and Grade 1 TEAE of hepatitis acute.

- ALT concentrations decreased to Grade 3 on Day 578, decreased further to Grade 1 on Day 585, and returned to normal on Day 595.

- AST concentrations decreased to Grade 2 on Day 578 and returned to normal on Day 585.

- Total bilirubin concentrations returned to normal on Day 578.

No participants had an elevated creatinine or low creatinine clearance (CLCR) value after the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date, some participants had CLCR that met a Division of Acquired Immunodeficiency Syndrome (DAIDS) graded abnormality due to a percent change from baseline not applicable for the paediatric participants in Study 304 who had low baseline serum creatinine. Minimum detectable changes in serum creatinine (0.1 mg/dL) in this population resulted in a large change in CLCR that was not clinically meaningful and did not represent changes in renal function. Grade 3 changes in CLCR observed after the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date were transient in nature and were deemed not clinically significant by the investigators. This is consistent with the CLCR abnormalities noted in the Study 304 Interim CSR. Of note:

- Cohort 3: 1 of 9 participants (11.1%) experienced a grade shift in CLCR from normal at baseline to Grade 3 after the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date.

- Creatinine values changed from 0.3 mg/dL at baseline to 0.5 mg/dL at Week 48, corresponding with a toxicity Grade 1 creatinine elevation. This result was not considered clinically significant by the investigator.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Cohort 4: 4 of 4 participants (100%) experienced a grade shift in CLCR from normal at baseline to Grade 3 after the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date.

- Creatinine values for each participant changed from 0.2 mg/dL at baseline to a maximum of 0.3 mg/dL during treatment and were not assessed as clinically significant by the investigator.

## Liver Function Tests (ALT, AST, Bilirubin, and ALP)

Clinically relevant elevations in LFTs were observed in 1 participant (Cohort 1) due to a TESAE and TEAE of hepatitis acute. Although the observed AST and ALT elevations met the criteria for consideration of treatment discontinuation according to the FDA Guidance Drug-Induced Liver Injury: Premarketing Clinical Evaluation (FDA 2009) (ALT or AST &gt; 3 × ULN and total bilirubin &gt; 2 × ULN), this participant was not discontinued, as fluctuations of LFTs were not consistent with a drug effect, liver enzymes began improving while berotralstat dosing continued, and the final diagnosis was acute hepatitis due to a viral origin.

No other clinically meaningful changes in LFTs were observed.

## Individual Grade 3 or 4 Lab Abnormalities

None of the clinical abnormalities were listed as clinically significant up until the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date.

- CLCR: 1 participant in Cohort 2 and 2 participants in Cohort 4 experienced a Grade 3 laboratory abnormality by highest grade. One participant in Cohort 3 experienced a Grade 4 laboratory abnormality by highest grade.
- Glucose, non-fasted: 1 participant in Cohort 2 experienced a Grade 3 laboratory abnormality by highest grade. The participant has the concurrent condition of Type 1 diabetes mellitus.

With the exception of the Grade 3 and 4 elevations in ALT, AST, and bilirubin associated with acute hepatitis, only non-clinically significant elevations in CLCR were reported as Grade 3 or higher after the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date.

## Hematology and Coagulation

## Laboratory Values Over Time

No notable changes from baseline were observed.

## Individual Participant Changes

A shift from normal hemoglobin at baseline to Grade 1 at worst post-baseline assessment was observed in a single participant in Cohort 2.

aPTT data were not collected until 08 May 2024 due to a protocol deviation. Since May 2024, no abnormal aPTT values have been reported. At least 1 aPTT value has since been reported from the central laboratory for 21of 29 participants.

## Individual Grade 3 or 4 Lab Abnormalities

One participant in Cohort 3 had an abnormal laboratory result of Grade 3 prothrombin time on Day 1, prior to treatment with berotralstat, which was not clinically significant.

## Urinalysis

Most abnormal laboratory parameters were a 1-grade shift from baseline. One of 9 participants (11.1%) in Cohort 2 experienced a grade shift in urine glucose from Grade 1 at baseline to Grade 3.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Individual Grade 3 or 4 Lab Abnormalities

One of 9 participants (11.1%) in Cohort 2 experienced a Grade 3 urine glucose laboratory abnormality concurrent with the condition of Type 1 diabetes mellitus. None of the observed urinalysis abnormalities were listed as clinically significant.

## Other Laboratory tests

## Vital Signs, Physical Findings, and Other Observations Related to Safety

## Vital Signs and Physical Examination

Vital sign data (ie, diastolic BP, systolic BP, pulse rate, respiratory rate, and temperature) remained relatively constant throughout treatment with no marked differences between cohorts up until the updated data cutoff date.

## Growth

It is noted that height and weight are likely to change over time in a paediatric population. As of the interim data cutoff date, no impact on growth and development in the paediatric population has been observed. No outstanding deviation of participants' height and weight development from the World Health Organization (WHO) Child Growth Standards have been observed. As of the 17 Sept 2025 interim data cutoff date for the CSR Addendum, no impairment on growth and development in the paediatric population had been observed. There was one participant with a Grade 1 TEAE of weight gain with noted crossing percentiles (from 87% to 99%) in a short period of time on the countryspecific growth and weight charts for children that the investigator felt was related to berotralstat. Berotralstat dose was not changed, and the TEAE started Day 336 with resolution on Day 546.

Dose changes occurred in Parts 2 and 3 to accommodate changes in weight due to participant's growth. Seven participants received dose increases due to weight changes during the study, including 5 participants who were initially assigned to berotralstat 108 mg oral granules in Cohort 2 and whose doses were increased to the berotralstat 150 mg capsule, and 2 participants who were initially assigned to berotralstat 96 mg oral granules in Cohort 3 and whose doses were increased to the berotralstat 108 mg oral granules. Ten participants across Cohorts 2, 3, and 4 reported dose increases due to weight changes after the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date.

Two participants in Cohort 1 experienced menarche while on berotralstat at Days 347 and 176. These participants were 10 and 11 years old at screening, respectively. No additional participants have experienced menarche after the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date.

## Electrocardiograms

Note: Collection of ECG data at Week 6 was added to the Protocol in Version 3.0. Nine participants completed Week 6 prior to this change and therefore were missing ECG data for Week 6. Additionally, ECG collection between 2 to 6 weeks for participants who underwent a dose change was added in Protocol Version 3.0.

Mean and median ECG parameters (HR, PR interval, QRS duration, QT interval, and QTcF interval), remained relatively constant throughout treatment up until the latest data cutoff date. The majority of results were interpreted as either normal or abnormal/not clinically significant. A single ECG was interpreted as abnormal, clinically significant for 1 participant in Cohort 3 at Week 6 (Day 40), with a description of 'tachycardia associated to flu-like syndrome'. The participant experienced a Grade 1 TEAE of influenza-like illness that started on Day 38 and resolved on Day 43, and the event was not related to treatment. Subsequent ECGs at Weeks 12, 24, and 36 were assessed as normal. Overall, no participant discontinued due to ECG abnormalities and no TEAE of QT prolongation was observed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Observed Changes in QTcF

There were no TEAEs for the TDP/QT Prolongation SMQ or palpitation PT up until the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date. Overall, the majority of the participants (22 participants [75.9%]) had a &lt; 30 msec increase from baseline to worst QTcF post-baseline. Seven participants (24.1%) had a ≥ 30 to ≤ 60 msec increase from baseline to worst QTcF post-baseline. No participant in the safety population had a &gt; 60 msec increase in QTcF or QTcF &gt; 450 msec.

No additional participants had a &lt; 30 msec increase or ≥ 30 msec to ≤ 60 msec increase from baseline to worst QTcF post-baseline after the 11 September 2024 interim data cutoff date. No participants had a &gt; 60 msec increase in QTcF or QTcF &gt; 450 msec.

## 5.4.2. Study BCX7353-114, Phase 1

## 5.4.2.1. Safety data collection

Safety assessments included monitoring of AEs, serious adverse events (SAEs), clinical laboratory tests, vital sign measurements, ECGs, and physical examinations (PEs) including skin examinations.

All subjects enrolled into the study who received a single dose of study drug (berotralstat) were included in the safety population. The safety population was used for disposition, safety, and PK concentration displays as well as listings of PK parameters.

## 5.4.2.2. Patient exposure

36 healthy subjects were enrolled to the study.

In Part 1 Periods 1 and 2, subjects received both a reference (Regimen A) and test (Regimen B) Formulation for the respective (single) dose, in a randomized sequence. Periods 1 and 2 were separated by a minimum 21-day washout period.

- Regimen A: 650 mg liquid formulation (reference)
- Regimen B: 675 mg mini-tablet (granules) formulation (test)

In Part 2, Periods 1 and 2, subjects received both a reference (Regimen D) and test (Regimen E) formulation for the respective dose for a 7-day period in a randomized sequence. In Period 3, all subjects received Regimen F. Periods D, E, and F were separated by a minimum 21-day washout period.

- Regimen D: 150 mg capsule formulation (reference)
- Regimen E: 150 mg mini-tablet formulation (test)
- Regimen F: 300 mg mini-tablet formulation (test)

## 5.4.2.3. Adverse events

## Most Frequent Adverse Events

The most notable difference between treatment groups was for GI events with 9/18 (50%) subjects in the 675 mg mini-tablet (Regimen B) treatment group reporting GI TEAEs vs. 4/18 (22.2%) in the 650 mg liquid formulation (Regimen A) group. Within the GI disorders system organ class, there was a large difference between the treatment groups with regard to diarrhea with 9/18 (50%) Regimen B

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

subjects reporting diarrhea vs. 1/18 (5.6%) Regimen A subject. Three of 18 (16.7%) Regimen A subjects experienced nausea vs. 1/18 (5.6%) Regimen B subject. The 2 treatment groups were similar for all other system organ class /PT categories with no more than 1/18 (5.6%) subject reporting any TEAE in either group.

The 150 mg capsule (Regimen D) and 150 mg mini-tablet (Regimen E) treatment groups were similar for all system organ class /PT categories with no more than 1/18 (5.6%) subject reporting any TEAE in either group. The 300 mg mini-tablet (Regimen F) treatment group had a higher number of subjects (5/18 [27.8%]) who experienced GI events as compared to subjects in either Regimen D or E (1/18 [5.6%] each).

The distribution of subjects within the GI disorder system organ class for Regimen F was: diarrhea, 2/18 (11.1%); flatulence, 2/18 (11.1%); vomiting, 2/18 (11.2%); nausea, 1/18 (5.6%); and constipation, 1/18 (5.6%). There were also 2/18 (11.1%) subjects in the Regimen F group who reported TEAEs in the nervous system disorder system organ class: 1/18 (5.6%) subject each who reported dizziness or headache.

## 5.4.2.3.1. Adverse drug reactions

The most notable observation was that diarrhea reported by the 9/18 (50%) subjects in the 675 mg mini-tablet (Regimen B) treatment group was determined to be drug related. In addition, the TEAE of nausea reported by 3/18 (16.7%) subjects in the 650 mg liquid formulation (Regimen A) treatment group was classified as drug related. There was no more than 1/18 (5.6%) subject reporting any other drug-related TEAE in either treatment group. The 150 mg capsule (Regimen D) and 150 mg mini-tablet (Regimen E) treatment groups were similar with no more than 1/18 (5.6%) subject reporting any drug-related TEAE in either treatment group. There was a higher incidence of drug-related TEAEs in the 300 mg mini-tablet (Regimen F) group, most notably in the GI disorders system organ class, with 2/18 (11.1%) subjects reporting diarrhea, 2/18 (11.1%) subjects reporting flatulence, and 2/18 (11.1%) subjects reporting vomiting. There was no more than 1/18 (5.6%) subject reporting any other drug-related TEAE in the Regimen F group.

## 5.4.2.3.2. AEs of special interest, serious adverse events and deaths, other significant events

There were no SAEs, including SUSARs, reported during either part of the study. There were no deaths reported in Study 114.

## 5.4.2.3.3. ADRs of special interest, serious ADRs and deaths causally related to the medicinal product.

For this protocol, nonserious treatment-emergent diffuse, pruritic, maculo-papular rashes (also called morbilliform rashes) were considered EOSIs (events of special interest). Other rashes and skin conditions not meeting these descriptive criteria were reported as adverse events.

One subject experienced a Grade 1 (mild) TEAE of macular rash (PT rash macular) starting on Day 10, 3 days after the last dose of berotralstat 150 mg QD mini-tablets. The event was considered by the investigator as definitely related to berotralstat. The macular rash resolved without treatment by Day 13.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.2.4. Vital signs and laboratory findings

In general, there were minor changes from baseline in the mean values for laboratory parameters over the course of the study; none of the changes from baseline were considered clinically meaningful. Two subjects had DMID Grade 3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities.

Three subjects experienced treatment-emergent DMID Grade 1 liver chemistry abnormalities. In 2 subjects ([Part 1] and [Part 2]), the elevations were observed at baseline visits immediately after the 21-day washout periods following the previous study periods. The third subject ([Part 2]) experienced 3 elevations, one in each of the 3 dosing periods, on the Day 10 visits following once-daily administration of berotralstat from Days 1 through Day 7.

No clinically significant changes from baseline occurred or were reported as a TEAE for vital signs. There were no clinically meaningful changes from baseline in the ECG parameters during the study.

## Assessment of Bowel Movements

Subjects in Part 2 were asked to complete a questionnaire from admission until discharge from the clinical research unit (CRU) describing the frequency of their bowel movements and to report and characterize any instances of diarrhea. Each bowel movement was to be rated according to the Bristol Stool Scale.

In the protocol-specified Assessment of Bowel Movements, the Bristol Stool Scale categories were combined into the following groups: normal - Type 3 or 4; constipation - Type 1 or 2; and diarrhea - Type 5, 6, or 7. This afforded the following distribution for diarrhea: 57.4%, 62.5%, and 57.3% for Regimens D, E, and F, respectively. However, consideration of the individual Bristol Stool Categories shows the protocol-specified diarrhea group (Types 5, 6, and 7) to be substantially weighted toward Type 5 (soft blobs with clear-cut edges) at the less severe end of the diarrhea group. Type 5 was the most frequently reported category across all 3 treatment regimens with an incidence of 38.3%, 43.8%, and 35.9% for Regimens D, E, and F, respectively. Type 7 (watery, no solid pieces) was reported for 12.2%, 11.8%, and 16.5% of Regimens D, E, and F subjects, respectively and is generally consistent with the 11.1% incidence of diarrhea reported as a TEAE.

## Taste and Palatability Assessment

Following each administration of drug product in Part 1, Cohort 1, subjects were asked to fill out a questionnaire describing the taste and palatability of the drug product. Subjects in Part 2 were asked to fill out a questionnaire describing the taste and palatability of the drug product following the dose on Day 1 and Day 7 for each treatment period.

The most notable observation in Part 1 was a substantially greater palatability for the mini-tablet formulation (675 mg, Regimen B) as compared to the liquid formulation (650 mg, Regimen A). Subjects consistently reported higher favorability ratings for the mini-tablet formulation with regards several evaluated parameters including aftertaste, bitterness, flavor, and swallowing experience.

In Part 2, which compared the palatability of the capsule formulation at the 150 mg dose level (Regimen D) to the mini-tablet formulation at the 150 mg (Regimen E) and 300 mg (Regimen F) dose levels on Days 1 and 7, the responses were balanced with subjects reporting similar favorability ratings for all 3 treatment groups across the Taste and Palatability categories evaluated. There were no notable differences in response between Day 1 and Day 7.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.2.5. Post marketing experience

Not applicable - the product is not licenced for patients &lt;12 years of age and therefore there is no post-marketing data available.

## 5.4.2.6. In vitro biomarker test for patient selection for safety

Not applicable

## 5.4.3. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical safety

## 5.4.3.1. Discussion

## 5.4.3.1.1. Overall assessment of available safety data

## Study BCX7353-304 (ongoing)

The safety database for the paediatric population was developed in accordance with the agreed Paediatric Investigation Plan (P/0151/2024), with a total enrolment of 29 participants from the study BCX7353-304. Overall, 93.1% of patients completed part 2 (48 weeks of treatment). The overall exposure was limited, totalling 53.5 patient-years of exposure (PYE) across all 29 participants as of the interim data cut-off date of 17 Sept 2025. The limited size of the safety database constitutes a significant limitation to drawing definitive conclusions regarding the safety profile, particularly for the lowest weight cohorts who experienced the shortest duration of exposure. Consequently, extrapolation from adult and adolescent populations is required.

The updated interim safety data presented from Study 304 (cut - off 17 September 2025) demonstrated that berotralstat has a manageable and consistent safety profile in children aged 2 to &lt; 12 years, comparable to that observed in adolescents and adults. The newly presented TEAEs were mostly mild, with four treatment - related events (nausea, vomiting, weight increased, headache) and no discontinuations, serious AEs, Grade 3/4 laboratory abnormalities, or deaths reported. Reported infections were unrelated to treatment and consistent with background paediatric rates. No hypersensitivity, anaphylaxis, severe cutaneous reactions, LFT elevations, or QTc prolongations were observed. Overall, no new safety signals emerged from clinical or post - marketing data to imply different safety profile in children.

Exclusion criteria included clinically significant cardiovascular abnormalities, moderate to severe hepatic impairment (ChildPugh B or C), renal impairment (creatinine clearance ≤ 30 mL/min/1.73 m² as per the Modified Schwartz formula), AST or ALT values at least three times the upper ageappropriate normal limit, or clinically significant ECG abnormalities. Comparing the Standard of Care (SOC) period to part 1, there is a substantial increase in the number of AE events from N=36 to N=61 with the percentage of patients reporting AEs also increasing from 62% to 72%. The system organ class category with the most AEs was Infections and infestations, the SOC and part 1 periods had a comparable number of AEs in this category. This was also the system organ class category with the highest number of AEs in each of the 4 cohorts. The imbalance in the number of AE events appears to be driven by a higher number of events in other categories including gastrointestinal disorders (N=8) respiratory thoracic and mediastinal disorder (N=7), and nervous system disorders (N=6) system organ class categories. Following queries, the MAH discussed that a similar imbalance was also observed in adult study BCX7353-302, and also with the placebo arm of that trial. It is therefore suggested to be due to an artifact of study design and differences in patient population. It is also noted that study 304 had more on-site and virtual meetings for part 1 compared to the SOC period, which may have influenced a higher number of AEs reported in part 1, while also potentially leading to higher bias recall in the SOC period.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The most commonly reported AE in the SOC period was nasopharyngitis (10% of patients), while in part 1 it was headache and upper respiratory tract infection (14% and 10% of patients). Throughout the study (periods 1, 2 and 3), the most common AEs were nasopharyngitis, upper respiratory tract infection, vomiting, headache, gastroenteritis and viral upper respiratory tract infection (35%, 28%, 17%, 14%, 14% and 14% of patients). Overall the majority of TEAEs were Grade 1 or Grade 2 in severity.  Headache and vomiting are listed as ADRs in section 4.8 of the Orladeyo SmPC with frequency 'very common' and 'common'. While nasopharyngitis, upper respiratory tract infection, gastroenteritis and viral upper respiratory tract are not listed as ADRs in the SmPC, all commonly occur in unmedicated children. Nasopharyngitis was also the AE that occurred most commonly in the pivotal study in adults (study BCX7353-302, 23% of patients).

As the numbers in each cohort were so small, it was difficult to discern whether or not there were any differences in the safety profile of AEs of interest across the cohorts. Similar safety profiles were related to berotralstat, nausea, headache, vomiting and weight increased. Nausea, headache and vomiting are listed as ADRs in section 4.8 of the Orladeyo SmPC. The related AE of weight gain (in a short period of time) is not listed as an ADR in the Orladeyo SmPC and is not considered a concern at amongst study 304 participants, and height and weight are likely to change over time in a paediatric population.

No events of special interest (EOSI) were defined in study 304. This deviates from the pivotal designated as EOSI, while gastrointestinal and hepatic events were identified as potential safety signals. In study 304, adverse events related to hypersensitivity, anaphylactic reactions, severe cutaneous adverse reactions, and cardiac concerns like Torsade de Pointes/QT prolongation were closely monitored. Hypersensitivity to the active substance or to any of the excipients is listed as a contraindication in section 4.3 of the SmPC, while rash is listed as an ADR in section 4.8 with frequency 'common'. Overall, there were n=9 (31%) patients who experienced 15 AEs of interest, 9 TEAEs were reported under the Hypersensitivity SMQ and 9 TEAEs were reported under the Anaphylactic Reaction SMQ. All were classified as mild or moderate, all events resolved while subjects continued berotralstat therapy without the need for dose modification and none were assessed as

observed by reviewing the data by 100 PYE (100 Person-Years of Exposure). Four AEs were considered present as this was a mild grade 1 AE which resolved, there were no other AEs of weight gain recorded In Study 304, HAE attacks and their associated symptoms were not classified as adverse events (AEs), including episodes necessitating hospitalization, which contrasts with the approach used in the berotralstat pivotal program for adults and adolescents. The clinical presentation of abdominal-only HAE attacks closely mimics gastrointestinal (GI) TEAEs, which poses a risk of differential misclassification between abdominal-only HAE attacks and GI TEAEs. Following queries the Applicant reaffirmed that there are important clinical differences between abdominal HAE attacks and gastrointestinal TEAEs, enabling patients to reliably distinguish the two. Key differentiators include pain characteristics, severity, duration, and response to HAE-specific therapy. Abdominal pain-typically crampy or colicky-was present in all HAE attacks, with most classified as severe (69.8%). In contrast, GI TEAEs such as nausea or diarrhoea occurred without such pain and were only mild to moderate (Grades 1 -2). HAE attacks required and responded to on-demand, HAE-specific treatment (90.3% showing expected response), whereas GI TEAEs resolved spontaneously without HAE medication and were shorter in duration. Patients and caregivers demonstrated reliable recognition of these patterns, consistent with previous findings across age groups. programme for berotralstat involving adults and adolescents where Rash/Hypersensitivity Events were related to the study intervention. Again, the small number of patients in each of the different cohorts meant it was difficult to discern whether or not there were any differences in the safety profile of AEs of interest across the cohorts.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

QT prolongation is listed in section 4.4 of the SmPC, however no PTs occurred in the TDP/QT prolongation SMQ or palpitation PT. Reviewing the data across all dosing cohorts in study 304, every participant maintained a worst post-baseline QTcF interval below 450ms, with no subjects exhibiting values above this threshold at any time. No individual demonstrated QTcF prolongation within the typically concerning ranges (&gt;450ms), including the more critical intervals above 470ms or 500ms. Most individuals experienced an increase in QTcF of less than 30ms from baseline, while a smaller proportion showed increases between 30 and less than 60ms. Overall, these findings indicate no clinically significant QTcF abnormalities within the studied patient population. All observed QTcF intervals and changes remained within accepted safety ranges. No subject discontinued due to ECG abnormalities, and no TEAE of QT prolongation was observed. There were no treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) reported for Torsade de pointes/QT prolongation standardized medical queries (SMQ), seizures, or palpitations. However, due to the very limited size of the safety database, this absence should not be interpreted as evidence that such events are implausible, particularly in patients experiencing higher drug exposures. The prescribing information includes a warning regarding increased risk of QT prolongation at elevated berotralstat concentrations, and it advises that children should not receive doses exceeding those recommended for their body weight. In Study 304, no QT - related signals were identified (all QTcF &lt; 450 msec), and the established adult/adolescent safety profile is unchanged based on post - marketing data through 2 December 2024.

Four SAEs occurred in the study to date. Two SAEs occurred in the same patient, accident and upper limb fracture, there was a grade 2 SAE of hepatitis acute and a grade 1 SAE of hemiparesis, none were not considered related to the IMP which is agreed. The SAE of hepatitis acute was considered by the investigator to possibly be due to a respiratory infection, as the respiratory sample was positive for rhinovirus/enterovirus, in the background of hepatic steatosis. The SAE of hemiparesis was considered by the investigator as likely a chronic undiagnosed condition, the patient had reported leg pain and abnormal gait with preference for tiptoe walking for a few years. No AEs led to treatment discontinuations during the study. Only one AE, affect lability, caused a dose modification, this was not considered related to the IMP. In addition, only one AE of hepatitis acute (the SAE described above) led to treatment interruption (33 days).

With regards to special populations, pregnant, breast-feeding and elderly populations are not relevant to the paediatric indication being pursued. No renally impaired or hepatic impaired patients were included in this study, however in line with the use of berotralstat, use in severe renal impairment should be avoided and use in moderate to severe hepatic impairment should be avoided.

No specific drug-drug interaction studies were performed, however drug-drug interactions are not expected to be different in adults and children.

Many subjects in the study exhibited laboratory results classified as 'high normal' or 'low normal,' particularly for serum albumin, creatinine, and creatinine clearance. Although there may be limitations associated with the application of reference intervals in this population, the laboratory data do not reveal any safety concerns or clinically relevant abnormalities in renal or hepatic function in the studied paediatric subjects.

Overall, no consistent trends or clinically significant changes in laboratory parameters were observed over the course of treatment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

No Grade 4 urinalysis abnormalities were observed. Most laboratory changes were 1-grade shifts from baseline. None were assessed as clinically significant. 1 patient experienced a grade 3 shift in glucose, non-fasted levels, however this case was confounded by the patient having type I diabetes.

The liver was the primary target organ for berotralstat-related toxicity in non-clinical studies. Clinically relevant elevations in liver function tests were observed in 1 subject in Study 304 due to acute hepatitis (SAE), which was of viral origin and not considered related to berotralstat, this patient was not discontinued from treatment and liver enzymes began improving while berotralstat dosing continued. No other participant had post baseline elevations in AST or ALT &gt;1.5 ULN.

No patient experienced a grade 2 or above abnormality in Hematology and Coagulation values post baseline. One participant in Cohort 3 had a Grade 3 prothrombin time abnormality on Day 1 before berotralstat treatment, which was not clinically significant. However due to a protocol deviation, aPTT values data were not collected until May 8, 2024, so baseline aPTT values are unavailable and no shifts from baseline cannot be assessed. Following queries, updated post-baseline aPTT values are available for 21 of 29 participants. No abnormal aPTT results have been reported.

Regarding vital signs and physical examinations, one subject had a grade 1 AE of weight increased as discussed previously.

One patient had a clinically significant ECG abnormality, this was a grade 1 tachycardia associated to flu-like syndrome and was not considered related to treatment. No abnormalities were assessed by the investigator as clinically significant, and no related events were reported as TEAEs. Plots of height, weight, and BMI over time for all cohorts showed no impact on growth or development in the paediatric population as of the interim data cut-off. Participant growth remained consistent with WHO Child Growth Standards, with no significant deviations observed.

There are a number of limitations with this study including the small sample size, although this is to be expected given the rare nature of the disease. The study was open label and may have been subject to unknown bias. The SOC period served as a control for safety assessments, however this was limited to 12 weeks in duration, was not a direct head-to-head comparison, and patients could be on any nonspecified SOC treatment.

Overall, no new safety signals were observed in the data presented to date, however the safety data are currently incomplete as the main trial remains ongoing.

## Study BCX7353-114

The new paediatric granules were also examined in a supportive adult healthy volunteer (n=36), phase 1 PK study. Collection of safety data was suitably reliable.

In part 1, the granule formulation was tested at a single dose of 675 mg, 4.5 times the proposed dose for this extension of indication application. In part 2, the granules were tested at a dose of 150 mg for period 1 and 2, and at a dose of 300 mg, double the proposed MAA dose, for period 3. Treatment compliance was very high as all subjects received their treatment on site.

In part 1, for the granules, the only AE which occurred in more than 1 subject was diarrhoea. While diarrhoea is a known ADR for berotralstat, the high incidence of diarrhoea (n=9, 50%) with this higher dose of 650 mg, together with all diarrhoea cases considered related to berotralstat, led to the discontinuation of high dose berotralstat as a potential acute treatment for hereditary angioedema.

In part 2, for the 150 mg granules, no AE occurred in more than one subject and the number of subjects with any TEAE was similar between the capsules and granules. The only related AE for the 150mg granules was rash (n=1) which is a known ADR for berotralstat and was mild in severity.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

For the 300 mg granules, the only AEs which occurred in more than 1 subject were diarrhoea, flatulence and vomiting, all of which were considered related to berotralstat by investigators and are known ADRs for the capsules with a frequency of very common (diarrhoea) or common (flatulence, vomiting). Additional AEs which were considered related included nausea (n=1), dizziness (n=1) and headache (n=1). Nausea and headache are ADRs for the capsules. While dizziness is not a known ADR for berotralstat, this related case is not of concern as it is a single case which occurred at a higher dose than is proposed in this current extension of indication application.

There were no SAEs, death or discontinuations due to AEs in study BCX7353-114. Only 1 AE of special interest occurred in the study, macular rash, as discussed previously.

There were only 2 subjects in the study who experienced grade 3 or 4 laboratory abnormalities, neither considered clinically relevant. The grade 4 event (elevated lipase) occurred in part 1 at a higher dose (650mg) than is proposed in this current extension of indication application. Elevated LFTs occurred in 3 subjects but were all grade 1 and in line with the expected safety profile of the capsules.

Results for taste and palatability were generally comparable across both capsule and granule formulations. Assessment of bowel movements were also generally comparable across both formulations.

There are a number of limitations with this study for safety assessment including the small sample size and adult healthy volunteers recruited (as opposed to paediatric patients). The study was open label and may have been subject to bias. In addition, this was a cross-over trial with a 21 day washout period, therefore any AE may potentially have been a long term carry over effect from the previous treatment period. However overall, no new safety signals were observed and these results further support the proposed new paediatric formulation and indication. The higher dose of 300 mg evaluated in this study suggests an adequate safety margin.

## Post-marketing data

The product is not licenced for patients &lt;12 years of age and therefore there is no post-marketing data available. Long-term safety in paediatric patients is currently captured as missing information in the RMP. To address this, a category 3 non-interventional PASS study is currently ongoing as a required additional pharmacovigilance activity. Studies 304 and 312 have been included in the RMP to address the long-term safety in paediatric patients.

## 5.4.3.1.2. Adverse drug reactions in the SmPC

The ADRs reported in the SmPC section 4.8 in patients 2 to &lt;12 years is generally the same as for the capsules for patients over &gt;12 years and this is agreed by CHMP. In addition, a new paragraph is proposed to be included in the SmPC for both the capsules and granules outlining the study 304 in children. It is agreed that all safety information is relevant for both SmPCs

## 5.4.3.2. Conclusions on clinical safety

Overall, no new safety signals were observed in the data presented to date, however the safety data are currently incomplete as the main trial remains ongoing.

The overall safety profile of berotralstat in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt;12 years appears consistent with that observed in adult and adolescent populations. However, it should be noted that the safety database for this paediatric population is limited, with an exposure of only 5.6 patient-years (PYE) in the lowest weight cohort. Additionally, due to the single-arm design and lack of a comparator or control group, the assessment of causality for reported adverse events is limited, and definitive causal inferences cannot be made.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The proposed amendment of section 4.8 of the SmPC is agreed and adequately describes the paediatric studies submitted in this application.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 6. Risk management plan

## 6.1. Safety specification

## 6.1.1. Proposed safety specification

No changes of the safety specification were proposed.

## 6.2. Pharmacovigilance plan

## 7.2.1. Proposed pharmacovigilance plan

## Routine pharmacovigilance activities

Routine pharmacovigilance activities beyond adverse reactions reporting and signal detection:

Specific adverse reaction follow-up questionnaire for any reported AE found in the TdP SMQ (broad PT terms): the follow-up form will specifically request information on independent risk factors for QT prolongation, both intrinsic and extrinsic.

Specific adverse reaction follow-up questionnaires for pregnancy: reported pregnancies will be collected with specialised forms to ensure all pertinent data are obtained. Attempts will be made to follow all pregnancies to outcome.

Specific  adverse  reaction  follow-up  questionnaires  for  paediatric  patients  (2  to  &lt;  12  years  of  age): reported adverse reactions and special situations will be collected with a specialised form.
