## 6.2.1. Discussion on the Pharmacovigilance Plan

## 6.2.1.1. Routine pharmacovigilance activities

Routine pharmacovigilance activities are acceptable.

## 6.2.1.2. Additional pharmacovigilance activities

The extension of indication includes patients between 2 and 12 years of age, studied for a short duration, the long-term safety should be further evaluated in paediatric patients in the post-marketing setting as acknowledged by the MAH. Therefore, BCX7353-304 ongoing study will continue to further characterise the safety profile and it is included as a Category 3 study in the RMP which is agreed.

Further in the ongoing PK and phase 3 Study 304, the participants will continue to be followed for up to 3 years. Physical examinations, and vital signs will be assessed at clinic visits. Age of menarche will be recorded for any female participant who experiences menarche during the study. In addition to recording the age at first menarche, it is considered important to examine whether there is added value in tracking the sexual maturity of all participants using Sexual Maturity Ratings (Tanner stages).

Since neither the extension / prolongation of Study 304 nor a registry were proved feasible in a post marketing setting, the MAH proposed that paediatric subjects who complete Study 304 would continue treatment in the ongoing Study 312. This is a multicentre, multinational, open-label study that offers the opportunity to patients with Type 1 or Type 2 HAE who have previously participated in berotralstat studies (i.e., Study BCX7353-204 [Study 204], BCX7353-302 [Study 302], and Study 304) to continue berotralstat treatment. Study 312 will continue for up to an additional 5 years or until paediatric patients reach 16 years of age. This proposal to follow-up paediatric patients in study 312 to characterise the long-term safety is agreed and mentioned in the RMP.

## 8. Product information

## 8.1. Summary of Product Characteristics (SmPC)

## 8.1.1. SmPC section 4.1 justification

## Orladeyo granules

The indication for Orladeyo granules has been amended to include a weight restriction in addition to the age restriction in children aged 2 to less than 12 years in Section 4.1. The lower weight limit of 15 kg has been justified by simulations based on the final popPK model and extrapolation to a weight limit of 15 kg is supported by a sensitivity analysis with model run6100 using fixed allometric exponents for weight.

The indication for Orladeyo granules has also been extended to allow for dosing in adolescents aged 12 years and older weighing less than 40 kg. This has been adequately justified by simulations based on the final popPK model and given that body weight is the key covariate in determining berotralstat exposure.

## Orladeyo capsules

The indication for Orladeyo capsules has been amended to include a weight restriction of at least 40 kg in addition to the age restriction in adult and adolescents aged 12 years and older. Therefore, the SmPC section 4.1 for the capsules has been amended to ensure consistency with the granules SmPC.

## 8.1.2. SmPC section 5.1 justification

## Study BCX7353-304

The MAH proposed to include efficacy endpoints for Study BCX7353-304 which reports descriptive efficacy endpoints only, as secondary objectives. The adjusted HAE attack rate per month Mean (±SEM) was the only efficacy endpoint proposed in Section 5.1. This is agreed on the basis that it can allow the prescriber to make a comparison to the pivotal data, and the sample size and statistical power is clearly represented.

## Normal C1-INH HAE (nC1-INH) population

In general, the strength of the data to support inclusion of results to Section 5.1 was questioned, as per EMA SmPC Guideline Rev.2 (2009), it is recommended to include main results (statistically compelling and clinically relevant) regarding pre-specified end points or clinical outcomes in the major trials. Hence, deletions to the proposed text were recommended, limitations to the selection of study population were included and the addition of ' This observational study suggests a similar trend in efficacy in patients with normal C1-INH HAE.' replaced the specific endpoints.

Further, wording was added to Sections 4.2 and 4.4 for patients with normal C1-INH HAE (nC1-INH) which is in line with other available agents indicated in HAE acting on the plasma kallikrein (PKa) pathway. Updates have been recommended during the Orladeyo renewal procedure EMA/R/0000282356.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The results of the user consultation with target patient groups on the package leaflet submitted by the MAH show that the package leaflet meets the criteria for readability as set out in the Guideline on the readability of the label and package leaflet of medicinal products for human use

## 9. Benefit-risk assessment

## Therapeutic context

## 9.1.1. Disease or condition, proposed therapeutic indication

Hereditary angioedema (HAE) is a genetically determined disease of dysregulation of the contact activation pathway. HAE is characterized clinically by recurrent episodes of angioedema of the skin, pharynx, larynx, gastrointestinal tract, genitals, and extremities.

Orladeyo (berotralstat; BCX7353) is approved in a hard capsule formulation for routine prevention of recurrent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in adult and adolescent patients aged 12 years and older.

## Molecular mechanism and presentation of HAE in paediatric patients

The molecular mechanism and presentation of HAE in paediatric patients are similar to adults. Both adult and paediatric patients experience recurrent unpredictable acute attacks of painful subcutaneous or submucosal oedema of the face, larynx, tongue, gastrointestinal tract, limbs, or genitalia which, if untreated, may last up to 5 days and can be fatal. The frequency and severity of HAE attacks show significant inter- and intra-individual variation in all age groups. In both adult and paediatric patients, the frequency of attacks can vary from rarely in some patients to every few days in others. Laryngeal oedema is less common in paediatric patients 11 years of age and younger compared to adults with HAE; however, HAE attacks involving the upper airway are potentially life-threatening regardless of age. Bork et al. have described fatal laryngeal oedema as the initial symptom in a 9-year-old male, and laryngeal oedema occurring in paediatric patients as young as 3 years. Nearly half of patients will have an upper airway attack by the age of 18 years.

The molecular mechanism of C1-INH deficiency is the same in adult and paediatric patients (mutation in SERPING1), resulting in deficient or dysfunctional C1-INH, which leads to elevated levels of the vasodilation signalling peptide BK and subsequently increased vascular permeability and contraction of visceral smooth muscle. Although phenotypic expression may not occur in the first years of life, the same laboratory assessments are used to establish diagnosis (C1-INH functional level and complement component 4 [C4]) in both adult and paediatric patients &gt; 1 year of age. Additionally, across different age groups, no developmental or ontogenic factors have been identified that might influence kallikrein levels. Therefore, the response to berotralstat treatment is expected to be the same in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years as in adult and adolescent subjects aged 12 years and older.

## Paediatric clinical presentation

The presentation of disease in paediatric patients with HAE is similar to adults. Both adult and paediatric patients experience recurrent unpredictable acute attacks of painful subcutaneous (SC) or submucosal swelling of the face, larynx, tongue, gastrointestinal tract, limbs, or genitalia which, if untreated, may last up to 5 days and can be fatal.

Laryngeal oedema is less frequent in paediatric patients aged 11 years and younger compared to adults with HAE; however, HAE attacks involving the upper airway are potentially life-threatening regardless of age.

The age at which attacks begin is variable, with rare reports of initial episodes of angioedema in the perinatal period. Symptoms can start as early as age one to two years. Approximately 40 percent of patients experience their first HAE attack before age 5 years and 75 percent by age 15 years, although repeated attacks in preadolescent children are uncommon. Thus, for the majority of patients, the disease first presents in childhood or adolescence. In a recent long-term survey of children and adolescents with HAE, initial symptoms occurred earlier than that described in the literature, that is, at a mean age of 10 to 12 years and time of the onset of the initial symptoms did not differ between the sexes. Attack frequency usually increases after puberty.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Long-term prophylaxis to minimize the impact of HAE on quality of life is seldom necessary under the age of 6 years, because HAE attacks occur less often at this age. For those who do require preventative therapy, the decision about which of these agents to use, if available, is based on safety, tolerability, and patient preference.

This application initially aims to amend the Orladeyo marketing authorisation to include four strengths (78 mg, 96 mg,108 mg and 132 mg) of film-coated granules as a new dosage form of berotralstat, administered orally, and to extend the indication for routine prevention of recurrent attacks of HAE to paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years.

## 9.1.2. Available therapies and unmet medical need

## Paediatric Patients Aged 2 to &lt; 12 Years Disease Management

Regardless of age, the management of HAE involves both treatment of acute attacks and consideration of prophylaxis to prevent attacks of HAE. In both of these categories, treatment options are limited for paediatric patients aged &lt; 12 years.

## Long-term prophylaxis (LTP) paediatric

Approved therapies for LTP in paediatric patients with HAE are limited to intravenously (IV) or SC administered plasma-derived C1-INHs, including Cinryze (IV infusion for routine prevention against HAE attacks in patients aged 6 years and older and for pre-procedure prevention of angioedema attacks in paediatric patients aged 2 years and older) or Berinert 2000/3000 (Haegarda, administered every 3 to 4 days in paediatric patients aged 8 years and older), as well as a monoclonal antibody inhibitor of plasma kallikrein (Takhzyro [lanadelumab], administered by SC injection every 2 to 4 weeks in patients aged 2 years and older).

Oral androgens (e.g., danazol), have numerous side effects that limit dosing, efficacy, and tolerability; in addition, the safety and effectiveness of danazol in paediatric patients have not been established. The World Allergy Organization/European Academy of Allergy and Clinical Immunology (WAO/EAACI) HAE guidelines do not recommend the use of long-term anabolic androgens for paediatric patients prior to Tanner stage V. Other oral agents used off-label in paediatric patients, such as tranexamic acid, lack efficacy data and are not recommended for prophylaxis per WAO/EAACI guidelines. However, in countries where targeted LTP is not available, tranexamic acid may be used off label for paediatric patients with HAE and preferred to off-label use of androgens due to its better safety profile.

## Acute management, paediatric

Approved therapies for the treatment of acute attacks in paediatric patients aged &lt; 12 years in the EU include an IV administered plasma-derived C1-INH (Berinert, no lower age limit, studied in age 5 years and older) and a SC administered B2 antagonist (Firazyr), indicated for paediatric patients aged 2 years and older).

## Unmet medical need

Current IV and SC administration in young paediatric patients may be challenging and potentially traumatic, affecting future compliance.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Therefore, there remains an unmet medical need for additional therapeutic options, particularly per oral route to prevent attacks of HAE in paediatric patients.

## 9.2. Main clinical studies

This application is based on paediatric extrapolation using pharmacokinetic (PK) and safety data to match effective and safe exposures in the adult (reference) population as substantial evidence in supporting the safe and effective use of berotralstat granules for oral administration in the paediatric population aged 2 to &lt; 12 years with HAE, in accordance with the agreed Paediatric Investigation Plan (P/0151/2024). Therefore, Study BCX7353-304 (Study 304), a Phase 3 open-label study was designed to evaluate the PK, safety, and effectiveness of berotralstat granuales for prophylaxis in paediatric subjects aged 2 to &lt; 12 years of age with HAE. Given the differences in study designs and subject populations, information from Study 304 has not been integrated with prior berotralstat clinical studies in adults and adolescents which the CHMP considered acceptable.

Study BCX7353-114; Part 2 only; was a Phase 1 relative bioavailability study of various berotralstat formulations (including capsules, granules, and liquid) in healthy subjects to evaluate relative bioavailability between the 3 formulations and the safety of berotralstat granules.

Furthermore, based on cross-study comparison of berotralstat exposures in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years to adult reference exposures (BCX7353-MS-003) and population PK simulations (BCX7353-MS-002), weight-based dosing at 4 dose levels of granules is recommended to achieve similar exposures in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years relative to adult exposures. The recommended doses based on body weight are 132 mg QD granules for patients with body weight ≥ 40 kg, 108 mg QD granules for patients with body weight 32 to &lt; 40 kg, 96 mg QD granules for patients with body weight 24 to &lt; 32 kg, and 72 mg QD granules for patients with body weight 15 to &lt; 24 kg.

## 9.3. Favourable effects

## Berotralstat film-coated granules formulation

The 150 mg berotralstat film-coated granules formulation demonstrated similar PK properties to the approved 150 mg berotralstat capsule formulation.

Study BCX7353-114 demonstrated bioequivalence between the granules and capsule formulation at steady state (Day 7) in a multiple dose study. The data collected in Study BCX7353-114 was considered acceptable to be implemented in the popPK model to predict the PK following dosing with the granules formulation for paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt;12 years with HAE.

## Study BCX7353-304 Efficacy analysis

Efficacy results have been presented through an interim data cutoff date of 24 March 2025 after all participants remaining on study had completed the effectiveness observation period (Week 48, end of Part 2). At the time of the interim data cutoff date of 17 September 2025 (CSR addendum), 5 participants had discontinued the study: 2 discontinued before Week 48 and 3 participants discontinued after Week 48. All participants remaining in the study (24 participants [82.8%]) were in Part 3 (Weeks 48 through 144). No participants had yet completed the study (Part 3). The evaluable population included an age range of 3 to 11 years old and weights ranging from 14.90 to 69.70 kg.

During the SOC period, 29 participants experienced a total of 135 adjusted HAE attacks across 4 cohorts: Cohort 1 (26 attacks), Cohort 2 (46 attacks), Cohort 3 (49 attacks), and Cohort 4 (14 attacks), with one participant in Cohort 1 reporting no attacks. In Part 1 (Day 1 to Week 12), 50

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

attacks were recorded, distributed as: Cohort 1 (20), Cohort 2 (8), Cohort 3 (17), and Cohort 4 (5), with 2 participants in Cohort 1, 5 in Cohort 2, 4 in Cohort 3, and 1 in Cohort 4 experiencing no attacks. Over Parts 1 and 2 (Day 1 to Week 48), 162 attacks were reported: 43 in Cohort 1, 51 in Cohort 2, 49 in Cohort 3, and 19 in Cohort 4.

Although the effectiveness analysis in study BCX7353-304 is descriptive in nature, collected effectiveness data suggested a trend of lower disease burden for subjects while on treatment with berotralstat relative to Standard of care (SOC), as demonstrated through:

- lower rates of attacks (median 0.96 attacks/month during the SOC period, 0.33 attacks/month during Part 1, and 0.417 attacks/month during Parts 1 and 2),
- Relative reduction from baseline in mean adjusted HAE attack rates for Week 12 completer population was 19% for Cohort 1, 58% for cohort 2, 36% for Cohort 3 and 70% for Cohort 4.
- Mean relative reduction from baseline for Week 48 completer population was 67% for Cohort 1, 38% for Cohort 2, and 62% for Cohort 3 and 50% for Cohort 4.
- a lower proportion of days with HAE symptoms (9% during the SOC period, 2% during Part 1, and 3% during Parts 1 and 2).
- berotralstat treatment produced a modest reduction in rate of attacks requiring targeted treatment (135 adjusted attacks were reported during the SOC period, of which 115 (85.2%) were treated with targeted acute HAE medication. By Week 12, 41 of 50 attacks (82.0%) in the completer population received targeted medication, with similar rates across all cohorts. During the Day 1-Week 48 treatment period, 127 of 162 attacks (78.4%) were treated with targeted medication.)
- lower rate of severe attacks (32.6% during the SOC period, 16.0% during Part 1, and 29.0% during Parts 1 and 2), and
- less professional healthcare was sought to manage HAE symptoms (22 attacks [16.3%] during the SOC period, 3 attacks [6.0%] during Part 1, and 9 attacks [5.6%] during Parts 1 and 2). No subjects were admitted to the hospital as a result of an adjusted attack in the SOC period or during Parts 1 and 2 of the study.

Trends observed during Parts 1 and 2 of the study are consistent across all cohorts and doses tested, indicating potential similar levels of effectiveness across the age and weight range included in this study. Discontinuations impacting efficacy outcomes were minimal.

The median duration of adjusted attacks was similar at baseline (SOC period), through Week 12, and up to Week 48. Berotralstat treatment did not discernibly alter the location of HAE attack symptoms experienced by subjects. The reduction in attack number and rate was generally similar regardless of attack location.

## Dosing recommendations based on Monte-Carlo simulations to justify berotralstat doses for paediatric patients

The popPK model informing dosing recommendations is considered fit-for-purpose. Simulations based on the final popPK model were used to select and justify dose recommendations for the posology of berotralstat in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt;12 years of age.

Study BCX7353-304 demonstrated that exposures following doses of 108 mg oral granules to 32 to &lt; 40 kg, 96 mg oral granules to 24 to &lt; 32 kg and 78 mg oral granules to 12 to &lt; 24 kg in paediatric patients 2 to &lt; 12 years of age result in exposures comparable to adults. These findings were supported by dosing simulations over a large number of doses and weight bands, indicating that the doses and weight bands studied in BCX7353-304 were mostly acceptable for these cohorts.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The updated proposed dosing regimen is outlined in Table 32 below from the SmPC.

Table 32. Recommended dose for children aged 2 to less than 12 years.

| Body weight (kg)         | Recommended dose of Orladeyo granules   |
|--------------------------|-----------------------------------------|
| 15 kg to less than 24 kg | One 72 mg sachet once daily             |
| 24 kg to less than 32 kg | One 96 mg sachet once daily             |
| 32 kg to less than 40 kg | One 108 mg sachet once daily            |
| 40 kg or greater         | One 132 mg sachet once daily            |

The recommended dose for adolescents aged 12 years and older with a body weight less than 40 kg is one 108 mg sachet of Orladeyo granules once daily.

## 9.3.1. Uncertainties and limitations about favourable effects

## Study BCX7353-304

The primary limitation of the clinical program for children aged 2 to 12 years lies in the open-label, uncontrolled design of Study 304, which provides only descriptive data and thereby has limitations in the statistical methodology. Limitations in comparing cohorts arose from small sample sizes, differences in drug exposure at the time of data cut-off, and the presence of multiple dose strengths within each cohort due to dose modifications during the treatment period.

Lack of PD data on kallikrein inhibition in the paediatric population is seen as a limitation of the programme, since there was no confirmation of the hypothesis that response to treatment is similar between paediatric and adult patients. The EoI clinical development is based on extrapolation approach with open label design , and efficacy being a secondary endpoint. However, given the rarity of the disease in the younger children, common pathogenesis of the disease between paediatric and adult patients and since descriptive efficacy data support reduction of the HAE attack rate, extrapolation approach based on exposure matching is accepted.

## Dosing recommendations based on simulations to justify berotralstat doses for paediatric patients

BCX7353-MS-02 presented a popPK model with the aim to characterise the PK of berotralstat in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt;12 years of age, and to use this model to perform simulations to support dosing recommendations in this group.

The MAH originally proposed a lower body weight limit of 15 kg, given the body weight range studied in Study BCX7353-304 (&gt;14.9 kg). However, according to the CDC growth charts, the typical body weight range for children 2 years of age would be approximately 10 - 15 kg, with average around 12 kg, and the MAH was requested to provide simulations with the same updated posology (72 mg QD) for the 12 to &lt; 24 kg group.

The MAH additionally presented the simulations based on the final model (run 5903a) for all paediatric weight ranges and their respective doses as on a continuous scale, with summary statistics (median, 5 th  and 95 th  percentile) binned by 2 kg ranges, allowing a closer inspection of exposure parameters than provided by the 12 to &lt; 24 kg range in the table. Based on the simulations from the final model, the Ctrough,ss for all weight and dose groups are in line with the reference range seen in adults. The simulations also demonstrate that although there is a trend of increasing median Cmax,ss with decreasing body weight, the median Cmax,ss does not exceed the 222 ng/mL limit for even the lowest body weight bin (12 to 14 kg).However, for the extrapolation outside of studied body weight range, the use of fixed exponents would be preferred as a more conservative approach. Even though the simulations using the model with estimated exponents confirm that median Cmax,ss values for 12 to &lt;15 kg group would remain below the threshold concentration of 222 ng/mL, in the sensitivity analysis using the model with fixed exponents the median Cmax,ss values for 12 to &lt; 15 kg cross this threshold. Therefore, as a measure of precaution, extrapolation for the 72 mg dose to the 12 to &lt;15 kg group is not supported. The lower body weight in the indication for Orladeyo granules is set to 15 kg.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Given that the mean weight of infants aged 2 years is approximately 12 kg it is considered important that the MAH further explores dosing in this subgroup of patients. It is therefore a recommendation [REC] that the MAH further investigate lower dosing regimens in paediatric subjects weighing 12 to &lt; 15 kg by performing additional simulations with lower dosing regimens for these weights.

## 9.4. Unfavourable effects

For the pivotal paediatric study, BCX7353-304, overall, 93.1% of patients had completed part 2 (48 weeks of treatment) for the main safety study at the most recent interim data cut-off date of 17 Sept 2025. Throughout the study (periods 1, 2 and 3), the most common AEs were nasopharyngitis, upper respiratory tract infection, vomiting, headache, gastroenteritis and viral upper respiratory tract infection (35%, 28%, 17%, 14%, 14% and 14% of patients). Overall, the majority of TEAEs were Grade 1 or Grade 2 in severity. Four AEs were considered related to berotralstat, nausea, headache, vomiting and weight increased. Nausea, headache and vomiting are listed as ADRs in section 5.1 of the Orladeyo SmPC with frequency 'not known', 'very common' and 'common' respectively. The related AE of weight gain was grade 1 and resolved.

No AEs of interest occurred connected with QT prolongation. AEs of interest in the hypersensitivity or anaphylactic reactions SMQs were all classified as mild or moderate, and none were assessed as related to the study intervention. Four SAEs (accident, fractured elbow, acute hepatitis, hemiparesis) occurred but were not considered related to the IMP which is agreed. No AEs led to treatment discontinuation during the study. Only one AE, affect lability, caused a dose modification, and only one AE, acute hepatitis, led to treatment interruption, neither were considered related to the IMP.

Apart from the SAE of acute hepatitis (mentioned above), no other safety concerns were raised in relation to laboratory abnormalities, vital signs or physical examinations. One patient had a clinically significant ECG abnormality; this was a grade 1 tachycardia associated to flu-like syndrome and was not considered related to treatment.

QT prolongation is an important potential risk for berotralstat doses higher than the recommended 150 mg QD in adult and adolescent subjects. Predicted geometric mean and median Cmax,ss values (≤ 165 ng/mL) are well below the 222 ng/mL threshold for a 10 msec QTcF increase.

## 9.4.1. Uncertainties and limitations about unfavourable effects

The safety database for this paediatric population is limited, with an exposure of only 5.6 patient-years (PYE) in the lowest weight cohort. However, the size of the safety database (n=29) is considered adequate given that hereditary angioedema is a rare disease and safety data is being extrapolated from the licenced indication in adults and adolescents. Additionally, due to the single-arm design and lack of a comparator or control group, the assessment of causality for reported adverse events is limited, and definitive causal inferences cannot be made. Nevertheless, the observed safety profile (in terms of event type, frequency, and severity) was consistent with the well-characterised safety profile of berotralstat in the approved adult and adolescent population, providing meaningful contextual reassurance in the absence of a formal comparator.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The study remains ongoing with only very limited safety data beyond week 48 presented.

Long-term safety in paediatric patients is currently captured as missing information in the RMP. To address this, a category 3 non-interventional PASS study is currently ongoing in adult and adolescent patients. While it is not considered feasible to include children 2 to &lt;12 years of age in this PASS study, study BCX7353-304 and study 312 have been included as a Category 3 study in the RMP for the missing information 'long-term safety in paediatric patients'.

## 9.6. Benefit-risk assessment and discussion

## 9.6.1. Importance of favourable and unfavourable effects

The extension of indication to paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years is based on matching exposure in paediatric patients to the exposure shown to be efficacious and safe in adult patients. Posology based on body weight is proposed in this regard.

Study 304 is the main source of efficacy and safety data for children 2-12 years. The robustness of the study is limited by the open-label uncontrolled design and only descriptive data, and as such the study results are only seen as supportive. Given the study design and the relatively low number of subjects included are considered acceptable.

Treatment with berotralstat resulted in a clinically meaningful and durable reduction in the rate of HAE attacks among paediatric subjects aged 3-12 years across all weight cohorts(ranging from 14.90 to 69.70 kg), with efficacy comparable to that previously demonstrated in the adult population. Overall, the results support the efficacy of berotralstat in reducing attack frequency in the paediatric population, while its influence on other attack characteristics (duration, location, and long-term severity) was limited.

Lack of PD data on kallikrein inhibition in the paediatric population is seen as a limitation of the programme, since there was no confirmation of the hypothesis that response to treatment is similar between paediatric and adult patients. However, given the rarity of the disease in the younger children, common pathogenesis of the disease between paediatric and adult patients and since descriptive efficacy data support reduction of the HAE attack rate, extrapolation approach based on exposure matching can be accepted.

The favourable effects regarding the proposed posology in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt;12 years weighing ≥ 15 kg with HAE are based on dosing and exposure data from the popPK model developed for paediatric patients with HAE which also takes into account that the geometric mean and median Cmax,ss values (≤ 165 ng/mL) are well below the 222 ng/mL threshold for a 10 msec QTcF increase .The modelling exercise also supported a dosing recommendation of one 108 mg sachet once daily in adolescent patients aged 12 years and older weighing less than 40 kg, a patient population not covered by the current posology for the hard capsules.

Overall, no new safety signals were observed in the data presented to date from the main paediatric study. The overall safety profile of berotralstat in paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt;12 years is consistent with that observed in adult and adolescent populations, and long-term safety will be further characterise in this sub-population through studies 304 and 312 added in the pharmacovigilance plan in the RMP.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 9.6.2. Balance of benefits and risks

## Pharmacology

The extension of the indication to include paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt; 12 years is based on exposure matching and Pop PK model is considered key to support posology in paediatric patients. The popPK model is considered fit-for-purpose to support posology in paediatric patients.

## Efficacy

Effectiveness data in Study BCX7353-304 indicated a clinically meaningful reduction in disease burden in subjects treated with berotralstat compared with SOC. These results provide supportive evidence for the efficacy of berotralstat in reducing attack frequency in the paediatric patients aged 2 to 12 years.

## Safety

No new safety signals have been identified to date in patients aged 2 to &lt;12 years of age. Long-term safety in paediatric patients is captured as missing information in the RMP. A category 3 noninterventional PASS study is currently ongoing in adults and adolescent patients and will provide longer term characterisation of the safety profile. Further, study BCX7353-304 and 312 have been included as a Category 3 study in the RMP for the missing information 'long-term safety in paediatric patients' including children aged 2 to 12 years.

## Posology

The agreed posology for paediatric patients aged 2 to &lt;12 years, with a new 12kg body weight cut-off limit would include the majority of patients aged 2 years according to CDC growth charts. However, additional simulations provided did not support extrapolation for the 72 mg dose to the 12 to &lt;15 kg group, given the median Cmax,ss exceeding the threshold concentration for QT prolongation. Therefore, as a measure of precaution, the lower body weight bound in the indication is 15 kg. In addition, the modelling exercise also supported posology in adolescent patients aged 12 years and older weighing less than 40 kg, a patient population not covered by the current posology for the hard capsules.

## 9.7. Benefit-risk conclusions

## 9.7.1. CHMP conclusions

The benefit-risk balance for Orladeyo granules in the routine prevention of recurrent attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in:

- children aged 2 to less than 12 years weighing at least 15 kg
- adolescents aged 12 and older weighing less than 40 kg

is positive.
